Exercise 3.14 In Exercises 3.12 and 3.14, we calculated the ground state of an isolated QCA cell, setting \(\Delta\) to a particular value each calculation. But, what determines the bias \(\Delta\) between the two dots? Several things could, such as an external electric field, or a nearby driver molecule. Let's focus on a driver molecule and treat it as a classical system that determines \(\Delta\) for a target cell through Coulomb repulsion. In this exercise, calculate \(\Delta\) for the target molecule as a function of driver polarization, \(P_{drv}\), assuming the driver molecule placed a distance \(a\) away from the target molecule can have any polarization \(-1\leq P_{drv}\leq1\). Also assume that each dot has a fixed (immobile) neutralizing charge of \(+q_{e}/2\) residing on each dot, where \(q_{e}\) is the fundamental charge, and the charge of an electron is \(-q_{e}\).

Figure 9: A neighboring driver molecule provides the classical bias, \(\Delta\). Fixed neutralizing charge of \(+q_{e}/2\) resides at each dot so that with one mobile electron, each molecular cell is charge neutral.

Show/hide solution...

The graphic from our solution is shown in Figure , computed using the code of Listing . The QCA cell insets were plotted using the drawDQD() function of Listing . We already know that \(P_{drv}=1\) means that the mobile electron occupies dot 1 for the driver, and that \(P_{drv}=-1\) means that the electron occupies dot 0 for the driver. What does a polarization \(-1 \begin{equation} \mathbf{E}_{drv}=p_{drv}\left(0\right)\mathbf{E}_{drv,0}\left(\mathbf{r}\right)+p_{drv}\left(1\right)\mathbf{E}_{drv,1}\left(\mathbf{r}\right). \end{equation}

The linear combination of fields for each state \(\alpha\) is weighted by the probability \(p_{drv}\left(\alpha\right)\). The rules of probability dictate that \(p_{drv}\left(0\right)+p_{drv}\left(1\right)=1\), so we can eliminate, say, \(p_{drv}\left(1\right)\) and write

\begin{equation} \mathbf{E}_{drv}=p_{drv}\left(0\right)\mathbf{E}_{drv,0}\left(\mathbf{r}\right)+\left(1-p_{drv}\left(0\right)\right)\mathbf{E}_{drv,1}\left(\mathbf{r}\right). \end{equation}

Recall that the potential energy between two charges \(q_{1}\) and \(q_{2}\) is given by

\begin{equation} \label{org85f6100} U=q_{2}\mathbf{E}_{1}\left(\mathbf{r}_{2}\right)=\frac{q_{1}q_{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}\left|\mathbf{r}_{2}-\mathbf{r}_{1}\right|}, \end{equation}

where \(\mathbf{r}_{1}\) and \(\mathbf{r}_{2}\) are the position of \(q_{1}\) and \(q_{2}\), respectively, and the field from charge 1 at some arbitrary point is

\begin{equation} \mathbf{E}_{1}\left(\mathbf{r}\right)=\frac{q_{1}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}\left|\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}\right|} \end{equation}

Now, let \(\mathbf{r}_{0}\) and \(\mathbf{r}_{1}\) be the positions of driver dots 0 and 1, and \(\mathbf{r}\) be an arbitrary position. We can calculate the field at \(\mathbf{r}\) due to the driver in the mean-field approximation:

\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} \mathbf{E}_{drv}\left(\mathbf{r}\right) & =p_{drv}\left(0\right)\mathbf{E}_{drv,0}\left(\mathbf{r}\right)+p_{drv}\left(1\right)\mathbf{E}_{drv,1}\left(\mathbf{r}\right)\\ & =-\frac{p_{drv}\left(0\right)q_{e}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}\left|\mathbf{r}_{0}-\mathbf{r}\right|}-\frac{p_{drv}\left(1\right)q_{e}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}\left|\mathbf{r}_{1}-\mathbf{r}\right|} \end{aligned} \end{equation}

Here, we have negative signs because we're assuming we have a mobile driver electron of charge \(-q_{e}\). In this mean-field approximation, we can treat \(-p_{drv}\left(0\right)q_{e}\) and \(-p_{drv}\left(1\right)q_{e}\) as effective fractional electrons residing on dots 0 and 1. In fact, we can write these as a function of the driver polarization. It can be shown that

\begin{equation} p_{drv}\left(0\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(1-P_{drv}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad p_{drv}\left(0\right)=\frac{1}{2}\left(1+P_{drv}\right) \end{equation}

Now, we can write an effective mobile charge on each dot:

\begin{equation} q_{0}=-\frac{1}{2}\left(1-P_{drv}\right)q_{e}\qquad\text{and}\qquad q_{1}=-\frac{1}{2}\left(1+P_{drv}\right)q_{e}. \end{equation}

Next, we calculate the electrostatic potential \(U_{0}\) of an electron on dot 0 of the target cell. The potential contribution \(U_{0,\text{fnc}}\) from the two neutralizing charges of the driver cell are

\begin{equation} U_{0,\text{fnc}}=\frac{q_{e}/2}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)+\frac{q_{e}/2}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{a\sqrt{2}}\right)=\frac{q_{e}}{8\pi\varepsilon_{0}a}\left(1+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right) \end{equation}

We must also calculate the electrostatic potential energy \(U_{0,\text{mc}}\) for the electron on dot 0 of the target cell due to the mobile charge on both dots of the driver cell:

\begin{equation} U_{0,\text{mc}}=\overbrace{\frac{q_{e}q_{o}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)}^{\text{driver cell, dot 0}}+\overbrace{\frac{q_{e}q_{1}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{a\sqrt{2}}\right)}^{\text{driver cell, dot 1}} \end{equation}

Thus, we can now write the \(\braket{0|\hat{H}|0}\) term of the Hamiltonian:

\begin{equation} \braket{0|\hat{H}|0}=U_{0,\text{fnc}}+U_{0,\text{mc}} \end{equation}

We can repeat this analysis for \(\braket{1|\hat{H}|1}=U_{1,\text{fnc}}+U_{1,\text{mc}}\). It can be shown that the potential energy on target cell dot 1 due to the fixed neutralizing charge is the same as the potential energy of the electron on dot zero:

\begin{equation} \label{org86df0fc} U_{1,\text{fnc}}=U_{0,\text{fnc}}. \end{equation}

Next, we calculate the potential energy \(U_{1,\text{mc}}\) of the target cell electron to be on dot 1 of the target cell due to the mobile charge of the driver:

\begin{equation} U_{1,\text{mc}}=\frac{\left(-q_{e}\right)q_{0}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{a\sqrt{2}}\right)+\frac{\left(-q_{e}\right)q_{1}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right) \end{equation}

Thus, if we want to calculate \(\Delta,\)we have

\begin{equation} \label{orgedbe644} \begin{aligned} \Delta & =\braket{1|\hat{H}|1}-\braket{0|\hat{H}|0}=\cancel{U_{1,\text{fnc}}}+U_{1,\text{mc}}-\cancel{U_{0,\text{fnc}}}-U_{0,\text{mc}}\nonumber \\ & \stackrel{\left(\ref{eq:DQD_FNC_potential}\right)}{=}U_{1,\text{mc}}-U_{0,\text{mc}}\nonumber \\ & =\frac{\left(-q_{e}\right)q_{0}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{a\sqrt{2}}\right)+\frac{\left(-q_{e}\right)q_{1}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)-\frac{\left(-q_{e}\right)q_{o}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{a}\right)-\frac{\left(-q_{e}\right)q_{1}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}}\left(\frac{1}{a\sqrt{2}}\right)\nonumber \\ & =\frac{-q_{e}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}a}\left[q_{0}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}-1\right)+q_{1}\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right]\nonumber \\ & =\frac{-q_{e}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}a}\left[q_{1}-q_{0}\right]\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\nonumber \\ & =\frac{-q_{e}^{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}a}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(\cancel{1}+P_{drv}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\cancel{1}-P_{drv}\right)\right]\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}-1\right)\nonumber \\ & =\frac{-q_{e}^{2}P_{drv}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}a}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}-1\right)=\kappa P_{drv}, \end{aligned} \end{equation}

where

\begin{equation} \label{org8bd6954} \kappa=-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}-1\right)\frac{q_{e}^{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}a}=\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\frac{q_{e}^{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}a}. \end{equation}

Notice that everything in \(\kappa\) is a physical or mathematical constant, save \(a\). Thus, \(a\) determines the value of \(\kappa\). We can now calculate \(\Delta\) and target cell polarization, \(P\), as a function of \(P_{\text{drv}}\), which we write only in terms of geometric and physical constants:

\begin{equation} \label{orgc1b41cb} \Delta\left(P_{\text{drv}}\right)=\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\frac{q_{e}^{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}a}P_{\text{drv}}. \end{equation}

Figure 10: Target cell polarization is plotted as a function of driver cell polarization, \(P_{\text{drv}}\). The driver is shown with a blue mobile charge, and the target cell is shown with a red mobile charge.


import os, sys
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

from qcapackage.qca.fourdotcell import FourDotCell as fdc
from qcapackage.qca.qcacircuit import QCACircuit as qckt
from qcapackage import constants as const
from qcapackage.quantum import linearalgebra as la
from qcapackage.quantum import computing as qc
from qcapackage.quantum import visualization as viz

# Constants
# Pauli matrices
# Note: Sz is in the quantum computing convention
Sx, Sy, Sz = qc.Sx, qc.Sy, qc.Sz
I = Sz@Sz

qe = const.qe # [C] fundamental charge
eps0 = const.eps0 # [F/m] = [C/V*m]

ket0, ket1 = qc.ket0, qc.ket1

ketMinus = (1/np.sqrt(2)) * (ket0 - ket1)

a = 1E-9 # [m] cell characteristic length

# I took out a factor of qe so that we end up
# with units of eV
kappa = qe /(4* np.pi* eps0 *a)*(1 -1/np.sqrt(2)) # [eV]

# Parameters
g = 0.05 # [eV] tunneling energy

D = kappa*1

nPd = 35
Pdrv = np.linspace(-1, 1, nPd)

Ptgt = np.zeros((nPd,))

for iPd, Pd in enumerate(Pdrv):
    D = Pd * kappa

    # Reference calculation - diagonalization
    H = -g*Sx - 0.5*D*Sz
    E0, psi0_ref = la.ground_state(H)
    Ptgt[iPd] = la.expectation_value( -Sz, psi0_ref )

# Upper left inset
Pd, Pt = -1, 1
ins_ul_name = os.path.join('img', f'inset_driven_dqd_{Pd}_{Pt}.png')
viz.draw_driven_dqd( [Pd, Pt], ins_ul_name )

# Lower right inset
Pd, Pt = 1, -1
ins_lr_name = os.path.join('img', f'inset_driven_dqd_{Pd}_{Pt}.png')
viz.draw_driven_dqd( [Pd, Pt], ins_lr_name )

plt.close('all')

fig, ax = plt.subplots()

ax.plot(Pdrv.flatten(), Ptgt, marker='o')
ax.set_xlim(-1, 1)

# Add inset images
inset_w, inset_h = 0.25, 0.25
inset_ul = viz.add_inset_image( ax, \
                        [0.05, 0.625, inset_w, inset_h],
                        ins_ul_name )

inset_lr = viz.add_inset_image( ax, \
                        [0.7, 0.125, inset_w, inset_h],
                        ins_lr_name )

ax.grid(True)
ax.set_xlabel(r'$P_{drv}$', fontsize=20, fontname='Times')
ax.set_ylabel(r'$P_{tgt}$', fontsize=20, fontname='Times')
plt.tight_layout()

fname = os.path.join( 'img', 'cell-cell-response-annotated_py.png')
plt.savefig( fname )

plt.close('all')

Exercise 3.15 Determine the cost of a bit flip, given a fully-polarized driver.

Show/hide solution...

Suppose that \(P_{\text{drv}}=1\). Then, Equation \eqref{orgc1b41cb} gives us

\begin{equation} \Delta\left(1\right)=\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\frac{q_{e}^{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}a}\left(1\right). \end{equation}

Because all the physical parameters and geometric constants are positive and \(1-1/\sqrt{2}>0\), it must be that \(\Delta>0\). What does this mean? Recall that \(\Delta\) is the potential energy by which \(\ket{1}\) is higher than \(\ket{0}\). This indicates to us that state \(\ket{0}\) is the preferred state for the target cell is if \(P_{\text{drv}}=1\), and state 1 is \(\Delta\) higher than state \(\ket{0}\). In other words, \(\Delta\) is the amount of energy which must be added to the system to force the target cell to take state \(\ket{1}\).
On the other hand, if \(P_{\text{drv}}=-1\), Equation \eqref{orgc1b41cb} gives us

\begin{equation} \Delta\left(-1\right)=-\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\frac{q_{e}^{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}a}. \end{equation}

This means that \(\ket{0}\) is energetically higher than \(\ket{1}\) by \(\Delta\left(-1\right)\) when \(P_{\text{drv}}=-1\). We must still excite the target cell by \(\Delta\) in order to make it occupy the state \(\ket{0}\) under these circumstances. In either case, the energy required to flip the target cell is the same, and we can interpret it as the cost of a bit flip. This is an important energy in the study of QCA, and we call it the kink energy,

\begin{equation} \label{orgc65d947} E_{k}=\left(1-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\frac{q_{e}^{2}}{4\pi\varepsilon_{0}a}. \end{equation}

We see here that two-dot QCA cells in the configuration of the driver-target system prefer to anti-align. If we were to make two adjacent cells align, this is called a kink, hence the term kink energy. For a cell with \(a=1\;\text{nm}\), we calculate \(E_{k}=421\;\text{meV}.\)

3.6. Quantum Information Processing

  • We've considered some operators now, from different contexts
    • the Hamiltonian, \(\mathcal{H}\), describes the physics of the system
    • other operators, such as \(\mathbf{X}\), \(\mathbf{Y}\), \(\mathbf{Z}\), and \(\mathbf{H}\) transform a qubit and may be thought of as gates for process quantum information
  • We now focus on quantum information and a way to visualize transformations of a qubit
  • To do this, we introduce the density operator and the Bloch vector
  1. The Density Operator

    • For a given state

      \begin{equation} \ket{\psi}\leftrightarrow\begin{bmatrix}c_{0}\\ c_{1} \end{bmatrix}, \end{equation}

      we can form the operator

      \begin{equation} \label{org148a00d} \hat{\rho}=\ket{\psi}\bra{\psi}=\begin{bmatrix}c_{0}\\ c_{1} \end{bmatrix}\left[\begin{array}{cc} c_{0}^{\ast} & c_{1}^{\ast}\end{array}\right]=\begin{bmatrix}c_{0}c_{0}^{\ast} & c_{0}c_{1}^{\ast}\\ c_{1}c_{0}^{\ast} & c_{1}c_{1}^{\ast} \end{bmatrix} \end{equation}
      • \(\hat{\rho}\) is called a density operator or a density matrix
    • Expectation values may be calculated from density matrices

      • For an operator \(\mathbf{O}\), the expectation value \(\Braket{\mathbf{O}}\) is given by

        \begin{equation} \label{org21cc8e1} \Braket{\mathbf{O}}=\text{Tr}\;\left(\mathbf{O}\hat{\rho}\right)=\text{Tr}\;\left(\hat{\rho}\mathbf{O}\right)=\sum_{j}\Braket{j|\mathbf{O}\hat{\rho}|j}=\sum_{j}\Braket{j|\hat{\rho}\mathbf{O}|j} \end{equation}
        • This expression is completely equivalent to the expectation value of Equation \eqref{orge3dbd25}

        • A property that sometimes comes in useful for the trace is that we can cyclically permute the operators in a trace and still get the same value:

          \begin{equation} \label{org4875196} \text{Tr}\;\left(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}\mathbf{C}\right)=\text{Tr}\;\left(\mathbf{C}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}\right)=\text{Tr}\;\left(\mathbf{B}\mathbf{C}\mathbf{A}\right) \end{equation}
          • We can do this as long as we move a single operator at a time from one end of the product to the other

        • For a product of two operators, Equation \eqref{org4875196} reduces to

          \begin{equation} \label{org78a6883} \text{Tr}\;\left(\mathbf{A}\mathbf{B}\right)=\text{Tr}\;\left(\mathbf{B}\mathbf{A}\right) \end{equation}

    Exercise 3.17 Write a Python function that converts a ket to a density operator.

    Show/hide solution...

    The solution code is given below: 

    
import numpy as np

# Constants
I = np.eye(2)
X = np.array([[0, 1], [1, 0]])
Z = np.array([[1, 0], [0, -1]]) # quantum information convention
Y = -1j*Z@X
H = (1/np.sqrt(2))*(X+Z)

ket0 = np.array([[1],[0]])
ket1 = X @ ket0

def densityOperator( psi: np.ndarray ) -> np.matrix:
  '''Transform a ket to a density operator (matrix).

  Args:
    psi: an numpy.ndarray representing a ket. For
         a single qubit, this will be a 2-by-1
         column vector.

  Returns:
    The density operator corresponding to psi.

  '''
  # @ is used for matrix multiplication
  # convert an numpy ndarray to a matrix
  # for access to the Hermitian adjoint
  return psi @ np.asmatrix(psi).H

# use the function with some test cases
rho0 = densityOperator( ket0 )

rho1 = densityOperator( ket1 )

rho_plus = densityOperator( H @ ket0 )

rho_minus = densityOperator( H @ ket1 )

print('\nDensity operator for ket 0 (type: {0}):\n{1}'.format(
  type(rho0), rho0) )

print('\nDensity operator for ket 0 (type: {0}):\n{1}'.format(
  type(rho1), rho1) )

print('\nDensity operator for ket + (type: {0}):\n{1}'.format(
  type(rho_plus), rho_plus) )

print('\nDensity operator for ket + (type: {0}):\n{1}'.format(
  type(rho_minus), rho_minus) )

plt.close('all')
    • Density operators have special properties. It can be shown that:

      • they have unit trace:

        \begin{equation} \mbox{Tr}\,\hat{\rho}=c_{0}c_{0}^{\ast}+c_{1}c_{1}^{\ast}=1 \end{equation}

      • they are Hermitian:

        \begin{equation} \hat{\rho}=\hat{\rho}^{\dagger} \end{equation}

        or, \(\rho_{ij}=\rho_{ji}^{\ast}\)

    • We can expand any single-qubit density operator over the matrices \(\{\mathbf{1},\hat{\sigma}_{x},\hat{\sigma}_{y},\hat{\sigma}_{z}\}\):

      \begin{equation} \label{org9c21364} \hat{\rho}=\frac{1}{2}\left(a_{0}\mathbf{1}+a_{x}\hat{\sigma}_{x}+a_{y}\hat{\sigma}_{y}+a_{z}\hat{\sigma}_{z}\right) \end{equation}

      with real coefficients \(\{a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a_{3}\}\)

      • For a simpler notation, we will define

        \begin{equation} \label{org745d72a} \hat{\sigma}_{0}=\mathbf{1},\quad\hat{\sigma}_{1}=\hat{\sigma}_{x},\quad\hat{\sigma}_{2}=\hat{\sigma}_{y},\quad\mbox{and}\quad\hat{\sigma}_{3}=\hat{\sigma}_{z}, \end{equation}

        and we rewrite our expansion as

        \begin{equation} \label{org1950c18} \hat{\rho}=\sum_{k=0}^{3}\frac{1}{2}a_{k}\hat{\sigma}_{k} \end{equation}

      • We can we can calculate the expansion coefficients \(\{a_{k}\}\) as follows:

        \begin{equation} \label{org57112bd} a_{k}=\mbox{Tr}\;\left(\hat{\rho}\hat{\sigma}_{k}\right)\stackrel{\left(\ref{eq:ExpectationValueDensityMatrix}\right)}{=}\Braket{\hat{\sigma}_{k}} \end{equation}
      • For any normalized \(\ket{\psi}\), \(a_{0}=1\), so this doesn't tell us much information.
      • On the other hand, \((a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\) are more informative
        • They will all have a value in the range \(-1\leq a_{k}\leq1\)
      • We can visualize the quantum state in 3D if we define the Bloch vector, \(\vec{\lambda}\), as a vector in real space (\(\mathbb{R}^{3}\))
      • To form a Bloch vector, we discard \(a_{0}\) and make a 3D vector using \(a_{1}\), \(a_{2}\), and \(a_{3}\)

      • Thus, in this example, our Bloch vector is

        \begin{equation} \label{orgcea4f64} \vec{\lambda}\equiv\begin{bmatrix}a_{1}\\ a_{2}\\ a_{3} \end{bmatrix}. \end{equation}

    • The Bloch vector components also may be calculated in state vector formalism as

      \begin{equation} \label{orgd4ce6a5} a_{k}=\braket{\hat{\sigma}_{k}}=\braket{\psi|\hat{\sigma}_{k}|\psi} \end{equation}

    Exercise 3.18 Write a Python functions to do the following:

    1. Convert a ket to a density operator
    2. Convert a ket to a Bloch vector See Equation \eqref{org148a00d}. Do your work in Python.
    Show/hide solution...

    The solution code is given below: 

    
import numpy as np

# Constants
I = np.eye(2)
X = np.array([[0, 1], [1, 0]])
Z = np.array([[1, 0], [0, -1]]) # quantum information convention
Y = -1j*Z@X
H = (1/np.sqrt(2))*(X+Z)

sigma = (I, X, Y, Z)

ket0 = np.array([[1],[0]])
ket1 = X @ ket0

def densityOperator( psi: np.ndarray ) -> np.matrix:
'''Transform a ket to a density operator (matrix).

Args:
 psi: an numpy.ndarray representing a ket. For
      a single qubit, this will be a 2-by-1
      column vector.

Returns:
 The density operator corresponding to psi.

'''
# @ is used for matrix multiplication
# convert an numpy ndarray to a matrix
# for access to the Hermitian adjoint
return psi @ np.asmatrix(psi).H

def BlochVector( psi: np.ndarray ) -> np.ndarray:
'''Transform ket psi to a Bloch vector.

Args:
 psi: an numpy.ndarray representing a ket for
      a single qubit. This will be a 2-by-1
      column vector.

Returns:
 The Bloch vector as a (3,) numpy array.

'''
rho = densityOperator( psi )

BV = [np.trace(rho @ sig).real for sig in sigma[1:]]

return np.array(BV)

# use the function with some test cases

BV0 = BlochVector( ket0 )
BV1 = BlochVector( ket1 )
BVplus = BlochVector( H @ ket0 )
BVminus = BlochVector( H @ ket1 )

print( f'Bloch vector for ket 0: ' + str(BV0) )
print( f'Bloch vector for ket 1: ' + str(BV1) )
print( f'Bloch vector for ket +: ' + str(BVplus) )
print( f'Bloch vector for ket -: ' + str(BVminus) )

import numpy as np

# Constants
I = np.eye(2)
X = np.array([[0, 1], [1, 0]])
Z = np.array([[1, 0], [0, -1]]) # quantum information convention
Y = -1j*Z@X
H = (1/np.sqrt(2))*(X+Z)

sigma = (I, X, Y, Z)

def densityOperator( psi: np.ndarray ) -> np.matrix:
    '''Transform a ket to a density operator (matrix).

    Args:
      psi: an numpy.ndarray representing a ket. For
           a single qubit, this will be a 2-by-1
           column vector.

    Returns:
      The density operator corresponding to psi.

    '''
    # @ is used for matrix multiplication
    # convert an numpy ndarray to a matrix
    # for access to the Hermitian adjoint
    return psi @ np.asmatrix(psi).H

def BlochVector( psi: np.ndarray ) -> np.ndarray:
    '''Transform ket psi to a Bloch vector.

    Args:
      psi: an numpy.ndarray representing a ket for
           a single qubit. This will be a 2-by-1
           column vector.

    Returns:
      The Bloch vector as a (3,) numpy array.

    '''

    rho = densityOperator( psi )

    BV = [np.trace(rho @ sig).real for sig in sigma[1:]]

    return np.array(BV)

Y = -1j*Z@X

    'BVplus': BVplus, 'BVminus': BVminus}

    • It may not be obvious, but we can think of an application of \(\mathbf{X}\) as a 180-degree rotation about the \(\hat{x}\) axis in the Bloch sphere.

      • We can make an arbitrary rotation through angle \(\theta_{x}\) about \(\hat{x}\) by forming the \(x\)-rotation operator from an exponential of \(\mathbf{X}\):

        \begin{equation} \label{org117986b} \mathbf{R}_{x}\left(\theta_{x}\right)=\exp\left(-i\frac{\theta_{x}}{2}\mathbf{X}\right)=\begin{bmatrix}\cos\left(\theta_{x}/2\right) & -i\sin\left(\theta_{x}/2\right)\\ -i\sin\left(\theta_{x}/2\right) & \cos\left(\theta_{x}/2\right) \end{bmatrix}. \end{equation}
      • We add a function for an arbitrary rotation \(\theta_{x}\) about \(\hat{x}\) to our file
    • Similarly, an application of \(\mathbf{Z}\) is a 180-degree rotation about the \(\hat{z}\) axis in the Bloch sphere, and
      • We can form an application of \(\mathbf{Y}\) is a 180-degree rotation about the \(\hat{y}\) axis in the Bloch sphere

    Exercise 3.20 Perform and rotations of \(\ket{0}\) about the \(\hat{x}\) axis through the angles \(\pi/4\), \(\pi/2\), \(3\pi/4\), \(\pi\), \(5\pi/4\), and \(3\pi/2\). See Equation \eqref{org148a00d}, \eqref{org57112bd}, \eqref{org117986b}.

    Show/hide solution...

    We'll do this in two steps. First, we'll make a file that contains our helper functions (the ones we wrote in the previous exercise). Then, we'll import and use those functions, saving the Bloch vector plots in files. The code is shown below, and the Bloch vectors are plotted in Figure 

    
import numpy as np
from scipy.linalg import expm

# Constants
I = np.eye(2)
X = np.array([[0, 1], [1, 0]])
Z = np.array([[1, 0], [0, -1]]) # quantum information convention
Y = -1j*Z@X
H = (1/np.sqrt(2))*(X+Z)

sigma = (I, X, Y, Z)

def densityOperator( psi: np.ndarray ) -> np.matrix:
  '''Transform a ket to a density operator (matrix).

  Args:
    psi: an numpy.ndarray representing a ket. For
         a single qubit, this will be a 2-by-1
         column vector.

  Returns:
    The density operator corresponding to psi.

  '''
  # @ is used for matrix multiplication
  # convert an numpy ndarray to a matrix
  # for access to the Hermitian adjoint
  return psi @ np.asmatrix(psi).H

def BlochVector( psi: np.ndarray ) -> np.ndarray:
  '''Transform ket psi to a Bloch vector.

  Args:
    psi: an numpy.ndarray representing a ket for
         a single qubit. This will be a 2-by-1
         column vector.

  Returns:
    The Bloch vector as a (3,) numpy array.

  '''
  rho = densityOperator( psi )

  # Refer to Equation 54
  BV = [np.trace(rho @ sig).real for sig in sigma[1:]]

  return np.array(BV)

def Rx(theta: float) -> np.ndarray:
  '''Creates a rotation operator through
  angle theta about the x axis.

  Args:
     theta: a rotation angle in radians
            about the x axis

  Returns:
     A rotation operator matrix

  '''
  return expm(-1j*0.5*theta*X)

def Ry(theta: float) -> np.ndarray:
  '''Creates a rotation operator through
  angle theta about the y axis.

  Args:
     theta: a rotation angle in radians
            about the y axis

  Returns:
     A rotation operator matrix

  '''
  return expm(-1j*0.5*theta*Y)

def Rz(theta: float) -> np.ndarray:
  '''Creates a rotation operator through
  angle theta about the z axis.

  Args:
     theta: a rotation angle in radians
            about the z axis

  Returns:
     A rotation operator matrix

  '''
  return expm(-1j*0.5*theta*Z)

def normalize( psi: np.ndarray ) -> np.ndarray:
  '''Normalizes a quantum wave function. This
  is designed for use with complex column
  vectors.

  Args:
     psi: a quantum wave function specified
          as a d-dimensional (d,1) np.ndarray.

  Returns:
     a normalized version of psi
  '''

  psi_norm_sq = np.asmatrix(psi).H @ psi

  # Refer to Equation 8
  return (1/np.sqrt(psi_norm_sq[0,0])) * psi

def direction( x: np.ndarray ) -> np.ndarray:
  '''Returns a unit vector pointing in the
   direction of a d-dimensional real-space
   vector x.

  Args:
     x: a d-dimensional [i.e., (d,)] vector

  Returns:
     a unit vector pointing in the direction
     of x.
  '''

  return (1/np.sqrt(np.dot(x,x)))*x

def labelPoint( ax, vec, label ):
  '''Helps us label a matplotlib axes
  on which a qiskit Bloch sphere plot
  exists.

  Args:
      ax: the axes containing the plot

      vec: the (3,) coordinates for the
          position of the label

      label: A string to define the label

  Returns:
      None
  '''

  # We have to interchange the x and y
  # coordinates and negate the
  # x coordinate to get this right.
  ax.text( vec[1], -vec[0], vec[2],
          label )

import os, sys
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

# Add path to QuantumInfoTools
cwd = os.getcwd()
pydir = os.path.join(cwd, 'python')
sys.path.append(pydir)

from qiskit.visualization import plot_bloch_vector
import QuantumInfoTools as qit

from QuantumInfoTools import I, H, X, Y, Z, sigma

ket0 = np.array([[1],[0]])
ket1 = X @ ket0

ang = np.pi*np.array([0, 0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 1, 1.25, 1.5])

kets = [ qit.Rx(th)@ket0 for th in ang]

vects = [ qit.BlochVector(ket) for ket in kets ] # make list of Bloch vectors

# fig and ax help us decorate the Bloch sphere
fig = plt.figure()
ax = fig.add_subplot(111,projection = '3d')
plot_bloch_vector( vects, ax=ax )
for idx, vect in enumerate(vects):
  qit.labelPoint( ax, vect, '{0:+4.2f} $\pi$'.format(ang[idx]/np.pi))

plt.savefig( os.path.join('img', 'RotateXvaried.png') )
plt.close('all')

    Rotations about \(\hat{x}\) are shown in Figure

    Figure 15: Various rotations about \(\hat{x}\).

    Figure 16: Various rotations about \(\hat{z}\).

    • Now we've learned to think about single-qubit transformations as rotations about axes in the Bloch sphere

    • Any single-qubit rotation can be expressed as a combination of rotation operations. Some examples:

      \begin{equation} \mathbf{X}=\mathbf{R}_{x}\left(\pm\pi\right),\qquad\mathbf{Y}=\mathbf{R}_{y}\left(\pm\pi\right),\text{\ensuremath{\qquad}and}\qquad\mathbf{Z}=\mathbf{R}_{z}\left(\pm\pi\right) \end{equation}
    • We also can make arbitrary rotations about arbitrary axes

      • Define the arbitrary axis

        \begin{equation} \hat{\mathbf{n}}=(n_{x},n_{y},n_{z})=n_{x}\mathbf{i}+n_{y}\mathbf{j}+n_{z}\mathbf{k} \end{equation}

      • Then, a rotation about \(\hat{\mathbf{n}}\) is given by

        \begin{equation} \label{org05d4fcf} \mathbf{R}(\hat{\mathbf{n}},\theta)=\exp\left(-i\frac{\theta}{2}\hat{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\vec{\sigma}\right), \end{equation}

        where

        \begin{equation} \label{orgc18f740} \vec{\sigma}=\hat{\sigma}_{x}\mathbf{i}+\hat{\sigma}_{y}\mathbf{j}+\hat{\sigma}_{z}\mathbf{k} \end{equation}

        is a vector of Pauli operators, and

        \begin{equation} \label{org52e7710} \hat{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\vec{\sigma}=n_{x}\hat{\sigma}_{x}+n_{y}\hat{\sigma}_{y}+n_{z}\hat{\sigma}_{z} \end{equation}

        is a single-qubit operator.

    Exercise 3.22 Find \(\hat{\mathbf{n}}\) to perform rotations about the following axes:

    1. The \(\hat{x}\) axis.

      Show/hide solution...\\

      If

      \begin{equation} \hat{\mathbf{n}}=\left(1,0,0\right), \end{equation}

      then Equation \eqref{org52e7710} becomes

      \begin{equation} \hat{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\vec{\sigma}=\hat{\sigma}_{x}, \end{equation}

      and our rotation becomes

      \begin{equation} \mathbf{R}(\hat{\mathbf{n}},\theta)=\exp\left(-i\frac{\theta}{2}\hat{\sigma}_{x}\right)=\exp\left(-i\frac{\theta}{2}\mathbf{X}\right). \end{equation}

      Notice that this is equivalent to the arbitrary rotation through \(\theta_{x}\) about \(\hat{x}\) from Equation \eqref{org117986b}.

    2. The \(\hat{y}\) axis.

      Show/hide solution...\\

      If

      \begin{equation} \hat{\mathbf{n}}=\left(0,1,0\right), \end{equation}

      then Equation \eqref{org52e7710} becomes

      \begin{equation} \hat{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\vec{\sigma}=\hat{\sigma}_{y}, \end{equation}

      and our rotation becomes

      \begin{equation} \mathbf{R}(\hat{\mathbf{n}},\theta)=\exp\left(-i\frac{\theta}{2}\hat{\sigma}_{y}\right)=\exp\left(-i\frac{\theta}{2}\mathbf{Y}\right). \end{equation}

      This is equivalent to the arbitrary rotation through \(\theta_{y}\) about \(\hat{y}\).

    3. The \(\hat{z}\) axis.

      Show/hide solution...\\

      If

      \begin{equation} \hat{\mathbf{n}}=\left(0,0,1\right), \end{equation}

      then Equation \eqref{org52e7710} becomes

      \begin{equation} {\normalcolor }\hat{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\vec{\sigma}=\hat{\sigma}_{z}, \end{equation}

      and our rotation becomes

      \begin{equation} \mathbf{R}(\hat{\mathbf{n}},\theta)=\exp\left(-i\frac{\theta}{2}\hat{\sigma}_{z}\right)=\exp\left(-i\frac{\theta}{2}\mathbf{Z}\right). \end{equation}

      This is equivalent to the arbitrary rotation through \(\theta_{z}\) about \(\hat{z}\).

3.7.3. Quantum Mechanics

4. The Variational Quantum Eigensolver for a Single, Driven QCA Molecule

  • You've already experience the Schrödinger equation,

    \begin{equation} \hat{H}\ket{\psi_{n}}=E_{n}\ket{\psi_{n}} \end{equation}
    • In QCA, we're often interested in the ground state, which is the lowest allowed energy of the quantum system

    • Classically, we find the ground state by diagonalizing the Hamiltonian and finding its lowest eigenenergy, \(E_{0}\):

      \begin{equation} E_{0}=\Braket{\psi_{0}|\hat{H}|\psi_{0}} \end{equation}
    • This corresponds to the global minimum energy
    • We call \(\ket{\psi_{0}}\) the ground state, since it has the lowest allowed energy, \(E_{0}\)

Exercise 4.1 Suppose we pick some \(\ket{\psi}\neq\ket{\psi_{0}}\). How will the energy \(E=\Braket{\psi|\hat{H}|\psi}\) compare to \(E_{0}\)?

Show/hide solution...

\(E\) must be greater than \(E_{0}\), since \(E_{0}\) is the lowest allowed energy of the system.

  • When quantum systems get large, the problem size gets large. Consider the following:

    • For a single QCA molecule, the state vector is a two-dimensional vector with a \(2\times2\) Hamiltonian:

      \begin{equation} \ket{\psi}=\left[\begin{array}{c} c_{0}\\ c_{1} \end{array}\right],\qquad\hat{H}=\left[\begin{array}{cc} H_{00} & H_{01}\\ H_{10} & H_{11} \end{array}\right] \end{equation}
    • For two QCA molecules, the state vector is \(2^{2}=4\) dimensional, with a four-dimensional Hamiltonian
    • For \(N\) QCA molecules, the state vector is \(2^{N}\) dimensional, with a \(2^{N}\times2^{N}\) Hamiltonian
  • When the problem size becomes large, it may be advantageous to avoid diagonalizing \(\hat{H}\)
    • This is where the variational quantum eigensolver (VQE) algorithm comes into play
  • The VQE algorithm uses three key steps:
    • A parametric quantum circuit to transform \(\ket{0}\) to \(\ket{\psi}\)

    • Measurement is used to calculate system energy for a quantum system

      \begin{equation} E=\Braket{\psi|\hat{H}|\psi} \end{equation}
      • If \(E>E_{0}\), then we haven't found the ground state energy
        • Since, in a VQE problem, we may not know the ground state, we don't necessarily know whether \(E\) is the ground state or not
    • We can try another wave function \(\ket{\psi^{\prime}}\), and compare its energy \(E^{\prime}\) to \(E\)
      • If \(E>E^{\prime}\) for some new energy \(E^{\prime}\), the we know that \(E\) is not the ground state, since we found an \(E^{\prime}
      • We don't know whether \(E^{\prime}\) is the ground state or not, so we can check another energy \(\ket{\psi^{\prime\prime}}\) and its energy \(E^{\prime\prime}\)
  • A classical optimizer modifies the parametric quantum circuit to generate a new \(\ket{\psi}\)
  • The procedure repeats until it finds a minimum \(\ket{\psi}\).

Exercise 4.2 A parametric quantum circuit. Consider the transformation of \(\ket{0}\) to state \(\ket{\psi}\):

\begin{equation} \label{orge13d89d} \ket{\psi}=\mathbf{R}_{z}\left(\theta_{z}\right)\mathbf{R}_{x}\left(\theta_{x}\right)\ket{0}. \end{equation}

  1. Find a combination of \(\theta_{x}\) and \(\theta_{z}\) so that \(\ket{\psi}=\ket{1}\).

    Show/hide solution...There are

    multiple solutions, but \(\theta_{x}=\pi\) and \(\theta_{z}=0\) could be used. Here is some code to illustrate this: 

    
import os, sys
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

# Add path to QuantumInfoTools
cwd = os.getcwd()
pydir = os.path.join(cwd, 'python')
sys.path.append(pydir)

from qiskit.visualization import plot_bloch_vector
import QuantumInfoTools as qit
import numpy as np

from QuantumInfoTools import I, H, X, Y, Z, sigma

ket0 = np.array([[1],[0]])
ket1 = qit.Rx(np.pi) @ ket0

kets = [ ket0, ket1 ] # make a list of kets - for iteration

vects = [ qit.BlochVector(ket) for ket in kets ] # make list of Bloch vectors

labels = ['ket 0', 'ket 1']

# fig and ax help us decorate the Bloch sphere
fig = plt.figure()
ax = fig.add_subplot(111,projection = '3d')
plot_bloch_vector( vects, ax=ax )
for idx, vect in enumerate(vects):
    qit.labelPoint( ax, vect, labels[idx] )

plt.savefig( os.path.join('img', 'ExerciseTransform0to1.png') )

plt.close('all')

    Figure 17: Transform \(\ket{0}\) to \(\ket{1}\) using \(\mathbf{R}_{z}\left(0\right)\mathbf{R}_{x}\left(\pi\right)\).

  2. Find a combination of \(\theta_{x}\) and \(\theta_{z}\) so that \(\ket{\psi}=\ket{-}\).

    Show/hide solution...There are

    multiple solutions, but \(\theta_{x}=\pi/2\) and \(\theta_{z}=3\pi/2\) could be used. 

    
import os, sys
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

# Add path to QuantumInfoTools
cwd = os.getcwd()
pydir = os.path.join(cwd, 'python')
sys.path.append(pydir)

from qiskit.visualization import plot_bloch_vector
import QuantumInfoTools as qit
import numpy as np

from QuantumInfoTools import I, H, X, Y, Z, sigma

ket0 = np.array([[1],[0]])
ketMinus = qit.Rz(3*np.pi/2) @ qit.Rx(np.pi/2) @ ket0

kets = [ ket0, ketMinus ] # make a list of kets - for iteration

vects = [ qit.BlochVector(ket) for ket in kets ] # make list of Bloch vectors

labels = ['ket 0', 'ket -']

# fig and ax help us decorate the Bloch sphere
fig = plt.figure()
ax = fig.add_subplot(111,projection = '3d')
plot_bloch_vector( vects, ax=ax )
for idx, vect in enumerate(vects):
    qit.labelPoint( ax, vect, labels[idx] )

plt.savefig( os.path.join('img', 'ExerciseTransform0toMinus.png') )
plt.close('all')

    Figure 18: Transform \(\ket{0}\) to \(\ket{-}\) using \(\mathbf{R}_{z}\left(3\pi/2\right)\mathbf{R}_{x}\left(\pi/2\right)\).

  • Hopefully, we now recognize that we can transform \(\ket{0}\) into any quantum state \(\ket{\psi}\) using only \(\mathbf{R}_{z}\left(\theta_{z}\right)\mathbf{R}_{x}\left(\theta_{x}\right)\)
    • All the information about our arbitrary transformation \(\mathbf{R}_{z}\left(\theta_{z}\right)\mathbf{R}_{x}\left(\theta_{x}\right)\) is contained in the two parameters, \(\theta_{x}\) and \(\theta_{z}\)
    • Thus, our circuit is called a parametric quantum circuit, since what it does depends on the parameters \(\theta_{x}\) and \(\theta_{z}\)
    • For a more compact notation, we sometimes define a parameter vector, \(\vec{\theta}=\left(\theta_{x},\theta_{z}\right)\) andrefer to our parameters collectively as \(\vec{\theta}\)
  • This is one part of the VQE process: a parametric circuit, called an ansatz, transforms our standard quantum state, \(\ket{0}\), into some other state, \(\ket{\psi}\), called a trial solution

    • We often use the symbol \(\mathbf{U}\left(\vec{\theta}\right)\) to reference our parametric quantum circuit, so that the process by which we transform \(\ket{0}\) to \(\ket{\psi}\) is shown in Figure 19

      Figure 19: An ansatz circuit prepares a trial wave function, \(\ket{\psi}\).
