6. The Variational Quantum Eigensolver for a A Pair of Driven QCA Molecules Molecule

7. Time-dependent Models of Quantum Systems

7.1. The Time-dependent Schrödinger Equation

7.1.1. An Isolated QCA Cell

  • Here, we consider an example of the time-dependent Schrödinger equation from Equation \eqref{org33cf856}

Exercise 7.1 Find \(\ket{\psi\left(t\right)}\) for a QCA cell prepared in state \(\ket{\psi\left(0\right)}=1\) if \(\gamma=50\;\text{meV}\) and \(\Delta=0\).

Show/hide solution ...

Assuming a constant Hamiltonian, \(\hat{H}\), e will use the solution of Equation \eqref{org21a80ba}. It can be shown that for \(\hat{H}=-\gamma\mathbf{X}\), the time evolution operator is given by

\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} \hat{U}\left(t\right) & \stackrel{\left(\ref{eq:TimeEvolutionOperator}\right)}{=}\exp\left(-i\gamma\mathbf{X}t\right)=\cos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hbar}t\right)\mathbf{I}-i\sin\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hbar}t\right)\hat{\sigma}_{x}.\\ & =\left[\begin{array}{cc} \cos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hbar}t\right) & -i\sin\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hbar}t\right)\\ -i\sin\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hbar}t\right) & \cos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hbar}t\right) \end{array}\right] \end{aligned} \end{equation}

Now, we can easily find \[\begin{aligned} \ket{\psi\left(t\right)} & =\hat{U}\left(t\right)\ket{\psi\left(0\right)}\\ & =\left[\begin{array}{cc} \cos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hbar}t\right) & -i\sin\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hbar}t\right)\\ -i\sin\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hbar}t\right) & \cos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hbar}t\right) \end{array}\right]\left[\begin{array}{c} 0\\ 1 \end{array}\right]\\ & =\left[\begin{array}{c} -i\sin\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hbar}t\right)\\ \cos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hbar}t\right) \end{array}\right]\\ & =-i\sin\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hbar}t\right)\ket{0}+\cos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hbar}t\right)\ket{1} \end{aligned}\] Is this reasonable? A quick check at \(t=0\) indeed confirms that \(\ket{\psi\left(0\right)}=\ket{1}\). We also note that \(\ket{\psi\left(t\right)}\) is periodic with a period, \(T_{0}\), that can be determined by setting

\begin{equation} \frac{\gamma}{\hbar}T_{0}=2\pi, \end{equation}

which yields

\begin{equation} T_{0}=\frac{2\pi\hbar}{\gamma} \end{equation}

At \(t=T_{0}/4=\pi\hbar/2\gamma\), we find

\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} \ket{\psi\left(\frac{\pi\hbar}{2\gamma}\right)} & =-i\sin\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hbar}\cdot\frac{\pi\hbar}{2\gamma}\right)\ket{0}+\cos\left(\frac{\gamma}{\hbar}\cdot\frac{\pi\hbar}{2\gamma}\right)\ket{1}\\ & =-i\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\ket{0}+\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)\ket{1}\\ & =-i\ket{0} \end{aligned} \end{equation}

We see that the system will oscillate between the computational basis states in what is called a Rabi oscillation.

Exercise 7.2 Find \(\ket{\psi\left(t\right)}\) for a QCA cell prepared in state \(\ket{\psi\left(0\right)}=\ket{0}\) if \(\gamma=50\;\text{meV}\) and \(\Delta=0\). Plot polarization as a function of time, and plot the energy as a function of time.

Show/hide solution ...

The code below calculates the time dynamics of \(\ket{\psi\left(t\right)}\) using a FOR loop. At each time step, we extract the polarization, \(-\Braket{\mathbf{Z}}\), and the total energy, \(\Braket{\hat{H}}\). The results are shown in Figure 33.

Show/hide code ... 

from scipy.linalg import expm
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import os

def dag( Q ):
    '''Calculates the Hermitian adjoint of a quantum object
       (vector or operator).

    Args:
      qobj: an numpy.ndarray representing a operator, bra,
            or ket.

    Returns:
      The Hermitian adjoint of Q.

    '''

    return np.conj( Q.transpose() )

# Constants
ket0 = np.array([[1],[0]], dtype='complex');
ket1 = np.array([[0],[1]], dtype='complex');

X = np.array([[0, 1], [1, 0]], dtype='complex');
Z = np.array([[1, 0], [0, -1]], dtype='complex');
hbar = 6.582E-16 # [eV*s] Planck's constant (reduced)

# Parameters
gamma = 0.05 # [eV] tunneling energy
T = np.pi*hbar/gamma # [s] time scale

# Initial state
psi0 = ket0

nt = 75 # number of time points
t = np.linspace(0, 3*T, nt) # time vector

# Calculations
H = -gamma*X # [eV] Hamiltonian

# Storage vector
Pt = np.zeros((nt,)) # polarization in time
Et = np.zeros((nt,)) # [eV] energy in time

for tidx, tval in enumerate(t):
    # Time-dependent state
    psit = expm( - 1j*tval*H/hbar ) @ psi0

    # calculate polarization
    Pt[tidx] = -np.real( dag(psit) @ Z @ psit )[0,0]

    # calculate energy expectation value
    Et[tidx] = np.real( dag(psit) @ H @ psit )[0,0]

fig, ax = plt.subplots(2,1)

ax[0].plot(t/T, Pt, marker='o');
ax[0].grid(True)
ax[0].set_ylabel(r'$P$', fontsize=18)
ax[0].set_xlim(t[0]/T, t[-1]/T)
plt.tight_layout()

ax[1].plot(t/T, Et, marker='o');
ax[1].grid(True)
ax[1].set_ylabel(r'$E$', fontsize=18)
ax[1].set_xlabel(r'$t/T$', fontsize=18)
ax[1].set_xlim(t[0]/T, t[-1]/T)
plt.tight_layout()

fname = os.path.join('img', 'cell-rabi-osc.png')
plt.savefig(fname)

print(f'Saved file as {fname}')
plt.close('all')

print(f'\n[[./{fname}]]')

Saved file as img/cell-rabi-osc.png

[[./img/cell-rabi-osc.png]]

Figure 33: Oscillation of an isolated QCA cell as calculated using the code of above.

  • We see the QCA cell oscillate between \(\ket{0}\) and \(\ket{1}\) with period \(T=\pi\hbar/\gamma\)
    • This oscillation is known as Rabi oscillation
    • \(T\) is called the Rabi oscillation period
      • This is the lower limit for electron transfer times in our QCA cell
    • This provides a physically relevant time scale
      • It is often useful to scale plots to a physically meaningful scale
        • This helps us avoid thinking about exact numbers, but rather more physically signficant quantities
  • We also see that total energy is conserved
    • The QCA cell in isolation is a closed system: it can neither gain nor loose energy
    • Why is the energy zero and not something else?
      • Recall that the diagonal elements of \(\hat{H}\) are potential energies
      • We can pick the zero of potential energy to be anything
      • In this case, we chose \(0\) as the average energy of states \(\ket{0}\) and \(\ket{1}\) way back in Equation \eqref{org7dafb9b}, and there is no bias (\(\Delta=0\)) for the isolated cell
      • Thus, both \(\ket{0}\) and \(\ket{1}\) have an energy of 0
      • We started with \(E\left(0\right)=0\), and so our closed system is fixed

7.1.2. A Pair of QCA Cells

Exercise 7.3 Repeat Exercise 7.2, but this time do it for an interacting but isolated pair of QCA cells.

Show/hide solution ...

This Python script calculates the time-evolution of an unbiased, isolated pair of QCA cells. The results are shown in Figure 34.

Show/hide code ... 

from scipy.linalg import expm
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import os

def dag( Q ):
    '''Calculates the Hermitian adjoint of a quantum object
       (vector or operator).

    Args:
      qobj: an numpy.ndarray representing a operator, bra,
            or ket.

    Returns:
      The Hermitian adjoint of Q.

    '''

    return np.conj( Q.transpose() )

# Constants
ket0 = np.array([[1],[0]], dtype='complex');
ket1 = np.array([[0],[1]], dtype='complex');

I = np.array([[1, 0], [0, 1]], dtype='complex');
X = np.array([[0, 1], [1, 0]], dtype='complex');
Z = np.array([[1, 0], [0, -1]], dtype='complex');
hbar = 6.582E-16 # [eV*s] Planck's constant (reduced)

# Parameters
gamma = 0.05 # [eV] tunneling energy
Ek = 0.421 # [eV] kink energy
T = np.pi*hbar/gamma # [s] time scale

# Initial state - product of states 1 and 0
psi0 = np.kron( ket0, ket1 )

nt = 125 # number of time points
t = np.linspace(0, 8*T, nt) # time vector

# Calculations
H0 = -gamma*X # [eV] Hamiltonian for cell 0
H1 = -gamma*X # [eV] Hamiltonian for cell 1
Hint = - 0.5*Ek * np.kron(Z, Z) # [eV] interaction term
# [eV] total Hamiltonian
H = np.kron(I, H0) + np.kron(H1, I) + Hint

# polarization operator for cell 0
Z0 = np.kron(I, Z)
# polarization operator for cell 1
Z1 = np.kron(Z, I)

# Storage vector
Pt = np.zeros((2,nt)) # polarization in time
Et = np.zeros((nt,)) # [eV] energy in time

for tidx, tval in enumerate(t):
    # Time-dependent state
    psit = expm( - 1j*tval*H/hbar ) @ psi0

    # calculate polarizations
    Pt[0,tidx] = -np.real( dag(psit) @ Z0 @ psit )[0,0]
    Pt[1,tidx] = -np.real( dag(psit) @ Z1 @ psit )[0,0]

    # calculate energy expectation value
    Et[tidx] = np.real( dag(psit) @ H @ psit )[0,0]

fig, ax = plt.subplots(2,1)

ax[0].plot(t/T, Pt[0,:], marker='o', label='Cell 0');
ax[0].plot(t/T, Pt[1,:], marker='v', label='Cell 1');
ax[0].grid(True)
ax[0].set_ylabel(r'$P$', fontsize=18)
ax[0].set_xlim(t[0]/T, t[-1]/T)
plt.tight_layout()

ax[1].plot(t/T, Et/Ek, marker='o');
ax[1].grid(True)
ax[1].set_ylabel(r'$E/E_k$', fontsize=18)
ax[1].set_xlabel(r'$t/T$', fontsize=18)
ax[1].set_xlim(t[0]/T, t[-1]/T)
ax[1].set_ylim(-.25, 0.75)
plt.tight_layout()

fname = os.path.join('img', 'cell-pair-osc.png')
plt.savefig(fname)

print(f'Saved file as {fname}')
plt.close('all')

print(f'\n[[./{fname}]]')

Saved file as img/cell-pair-osc.png

7.2. The Quantum Liouville Equation

  • Equvialent to the time-dependent Schrödinger is the quantum Liouville equation,

    \begin{equation} \label{orgedc5496} \frac{d\hat{\rho}}{dt}=-\frac{i}{\hbar}\left[\hat{H},\hat{\rho}\right], \end{equation}

    where \(\left[\hat{A},\hat{B}\right]\) is the commutator:

    \begin{equation} \label{org953ca93} \left[\hat{A},\hat{B}\right]=\hat{A}\hat{B}-\hat{B}\hat{A} \end{equation}
  • Though Equation \eqref{orgedc5496} is equivalent to Equation \eqref{org33cf856} as a description of a closed quantum system, our interest in Equation \eqref{orgedc5496} lies in the fact that it may be extended to model open systems.

7.2.1. Pythonic Calculations of Quantum Liouvillian Evolution

  • To solve the quantum Liouville equation in Python, we need a package that integrates solutions to not just matrix differential equations, but also complex matrix differential equations
  • The odeintw package provides these capabilities

Exercise 7.4 Use Python to solve the quantum Liouville equation for the dynamics of an isolated QCA cell. Plot the expectation value of energy as a function of time, as well as the cell polarization. Compare your result to dynamics calculated using the Schrödinger equation.

Show/hide solution ...

The code given below is one approach to this problem. Results are shown in Figure 35, in which we see that the quantum Liouville model produces the same result as the Schrödinger model.

Show/hide code ... 

from odeintw import odeintw
import numpy as np
import scipy as sp
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import os

# Constants
hbar = 6.58E-16 # [eV*s] the reduced Planck constant
ket0 = np.array([[1],[0]], dtype='complex')
Sx = np.array([[0, 1],[1, 0]], dtype='complex')
Sz = np.array([[1, 0],[0, -1]], dtype='complex')

def expectation_value( op, psi ):
  """
     Return the expectation value for an
     operator op.

     Args
       psi: a state vector or a density matrix

     Returns real expectation value
  """

  if psi.shape[0] == psi.shape[1]: # if psi is a density matrix
      result = np.trace( op @ psi ).real

  else: # if psi is a vector

      temp = dag( psi ) @ op @ psi
      result = temp[0,0].real

  return result

def dag( Q ):
  '''Calculates the Hermitian adjoint of a quantum object
     (vector or operator).

  Args:
    qobj: an numpy.ndarray representing a operator, bra,
          or ket.

  Returns:
    The Hermitian adjoint of Q.

  '''

  return np.conj( Q.transpose() )

def commutator( A, B ):
  """
     Evaluates the commutator of two operators.
  """
  return A @ B - B @ A

def quantum_liouville(rho, t, H_constant):
  """
     Implement the quantum Liouville equation for
     a constant Hamiltonian

     For use with the odeintw.odeintw() function
  """
  drhodt = -(1j/hbar)*commutator(H_constant, rho)

  return drhodt

# Parameters
nt = 75 # [] number of time steps
gamma = 0.025 # [eV] tunneling energy

# Calculations
T = np.pi*hbar/gamma # time scale
t = np.linspace(0, 4*T, nt)
H = -gamma*Sx # [eV] Hamiltonian

psi0 = ket0 # initial state - state vector

rho0 = psi0 @ dag( psi0 )

# Integrate the solution for the quantum Liouville equation
sol = odeintw(quantum_liouville, rho0, t, args=(H,) )

Pt_ode = np.zeros([nt,])
Pt_exp = np.zeros([nt,])
Et_ode = np.zeros((nt,))
Et_exp = np.zeros((nt,))

# Extract data from ode solution; also calculate Schrodinger version
for it in range(nt):
  # Density matrix - extract data
  Pt_ode[it] = expectation_value(-Sz, sol[it])
  Et_ode[it] = expectation_value(H, sol[it])

  # State vector - calculate evolution, extract data
  Ut = sp.linalg.expm(-1j*t[it]*H/hbar)
  psit = Ut @ psi0
  Pt_exp[it] = expectation_value(-Sz, psit)
  Et_exp[it] = expectation_value(H, psit)

fig, ax = plt.subplots(2,1)

ax[0].plot(t/T, Pt_exp, label='State vector', marker='v')
ax[0].plot(t/T, Pt_ode, label='Density matrix', marker='o',
         linestyle='dashdot')
ax[0].set_xlim(t[0]/T, t[-1]/T)
ax[0].legend()
ax[0].grid(True)
# ax.set_xlabel(r'$t/T$', fontsize=16)
ax[0].set_ylabel(r'$P$', fontsize=16)
plt.tight_layout()

ax[1].plot(t/T, Et_exp*1E3, label='State vector', marker='v')
ax[1].plot(t/T, Et_ode*1E3, label='Density matrix', marker='o',
         linestyle='dashdot')
ax[1].set_xlim(t[0]/T, t[-1]/T)
ax[1].legend()
ax[1].grid(True)
ax[1].set_xlabel(r'$t/T$', fontsize=16)
ax[1].set_ylabel(r'$E$ (meV)', fontsize=16)
plt.tight_layout()

fname = os.path.join('img', 'qca-quantLiouville-Pt.png')
plt.savefig(fname)

plt.close("all")
print(f'\n[[./{fname}]]')


[[./img/qca-quantLiouville-Pt.png]]

Figure 35: A state vector model matches a density operator model of the dynamics of an isolated QCA cell. The code that produced this is given in the code listing above.

8. The Quantum Harmonic Oscillator

  • The quantum harmonic oscillator is an important system in quantum mechanics

  • The time-independent Schrödinger equation may be written as

    \begin{equation} -\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\frac{d^{2}\psi}{dx^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}m\omega^{2}x^{2}\psi=E\psi, \end{equation}

    where \(x\) is the oscillator position

  • In this system, we identify the Hamiltonian as

    \begin{equation} \hat{H}=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\frac{d^{2}}{dx^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}m\omega^{2}x^{2}, \end{equation}

    so that we can write

    \begin{equation} \label{org2147cf1} \hat{H}\psi_{n}\left(x\right)=E_{n}\psi_{n}\left(x\right) \end{equation}

    • We may also write this using Dirac's bra-ket formalism:

      \begin{equation} \label{orgbe252d0} \hat{H}\ket{\psi_{n}}=E_{n}\ket{\psi_{n}} \end{equation}

  • It may be shown that the functions \(\psi_{n}\left(x\right)\)–called eigenfunctions of \(\hat{H}\), each with its own energy \(E_{n}\)– have energies given by

    \begin{equation} E_{n}=\hbar\omega\left(n+\frac{1}{2}\right),\qquad n\geq0 \end{equation}
    • The eigenenergies are evenly spaced in energy
      • \(E_{0}=\hbar\omega/2\) is the ground state energy associated with \(\psi_{0}\left(x\right)\), which is known as the ground state
        • The ground state is the lowest allowed energy for the quantum harmonic oscillator
      • The state \(\psi_{1}\left(x\right)\) with \(E_{1}=3\hbar\omega/2\) is called the first excited state
      • The state \(\psi_{2}\left(x\right)\) with \(E_{2}=5\hbar\omega/2\) is called the second excited state
      • The energy spacing, \(\hbar\omega\) between states \(\psi_{n}\left(x\right)\) and \(\psi_{n\pm1}\left(x\right)\) is called the energy quantization
        • This is a quantum of energy, also called a vibron
        • In semiconductors, these quanta are called phonons
    • There is an infinte number of eigenfunctions and eigenenergies: \(n\in\left\{ 0,1,2,\ldots\right\}\)

  • The general quantum state of a quantum harmonic oscillator may be a superposition of eigenstates:

    \begin{equation} \label{orga3ac984} \psi\left(x\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}c_{n}\psi_{n}\left(x\right) \end{equation}
    • Here, the eigenfunctions \(\left\{ \psi_{n}\left(x\right)\right\}\) form an infinite basis for the quantum state \(\psi\left(x\right)\)
    • In practice, contributions to \(\psi\left(x\right)\) from lower values of \(n\) are most significant, and \(\left|c_{n}\right|\) for large \(n\) becomes very small
    • In this expansion, we are using the energy eigenstates as a basis for an arbitrary \(\psi\left(x\right)\)
      • We refer to this as the energy basis or the Fock basis

  • We can define position and momentum operators,

    \begin{equation} \hat{x}=x,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\hat{p}=i\hbar\frac{d}{dx} \end{equation}

    • Thus, we can write the Hamiltonian as

      \begin{equation} \hat{H}=\frac{\hat{p}^{2}}{2m}+\frac{1}{2}m\omega\hat{x}^{2} \end{equation}

  • We also may define creation and annihilation operators,

    \begin{equation} \hat{a}^{\dagger}=\sqrt{\frac{m\omega}{2\hbar}}\left(\hat{x}+\frac{i}{m\omega}\hat{p}\right)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\hat{a}=\sqrt{\frac{m\omega}{2\hbar}}\left(\hat{x}-\frac{i}{m\omega}\hat{p}\right) \end{equation}

    • We also can write the Hamiltonian in terms of these:

      \begin{equation} \hat{H}=\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\left(\hbar\omega+\frac{1}{2}\right) \end{equation}

    • We can write the position and momentum operators in terms of \(\hat{a}\) and \(\hat{a}^{\dagger}\):

      \begin{equation} \label{org21a42a9} \begin{aligned} \hat{x} & =\sqrt{\frac{\hbar}{2m\omega}}\left(\hat{a}^{\dagger}+\hat{a}\right),\\ \hat{p} & =\sqrt{\frac{\hbar m\omega}{2}}\left(\hat{a}^{\dagger}-\hat{a}\right) \end{aligned} \end{equation}

    • The operator \(\hat{a}\) transforms an eigenstate \(\psi_{n}\left(x\right)\) as follows:

      \begin{equation} \label{orge83ebde} \hat{a}\psi_{n}\left(x\right)=\begin{cases} \sqrt{n}\psi_{n-1}\left(x\right), & n>0\\ 0, & n=0 \end{cases} \end{equation}
      • \(\hat{a}\) reduces the state's energy by one vibron. In other words, it annihilates one quanta of vibration, so we call this operator the annihilation operator

    • The operator \(\hat{a}^{\dagger}\) transforms an eigenstate \(\psi_{n}\left(x\right)\) as follows:

      \begin{equation} \label{org907e3d7} \hat{a}^{\dagger}\psi_{n}\left(x\right)=\sqrt{n+1}\psi_{n+1}\left(x\right) \end{equation}
      • \(\hat{a}\) increases the state's energy by one vibron. In other words, it creates one quanta of vibration, so we call this operator the creation operator

  • Consider the action of \(\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\) on a state \(\psi_{n}\left(x\right)\):

    \begin{equation} \label{orgbafa4c5} \hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\psi_{n}\left(x\right)=\sqrt{n}\hat{a}^{\dagger}\psi_{n-1}\left(x\right)=n\psi_{n}\left(x\right) \end{equation}
    • The action of \(\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\) is to extract \(n\), the number of quanta in the state \(\psi_{n}\left(n\right)\)

    • Thus, we define \(\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}\) as the number operator, \(\hat{n}\):

      \begin{equation} \label{orgba8f669} \hat{n}=\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a} \end{equation}
    • The integer \(n\) is also called the occupation number, because it tells us how many quanta of vibration exist in a quantum state

8.1. Computer Modeling of the QHO

8.1.1. The Energy Basis Representation for the QHO

  • We start by truncating our infinite basis to a basis with \(N\) elements, \(\left\{ \psi_{0}\left(x\right),\psi_{1}\left(x\right),\ldots,\psi_{N-1}\left(x\right)\right\}\), so we have an approximation to \(\psi\left(x\right)\):

    \begin{equation} \psi\left(x\right)\simeq\sum_{n=0}^{N}c_{n}\psi_{n}\left(x\right) \end{equation}

  • Next, we assign elementary vectors to each eigenstate in our truncated basis:

    \begin{equation} \psi_{0}\left(x\right)\leftrightarrow\vec{e}_{1}=\left[\begin{array}{c} 1\\ 0\\ 0\\ \vdots \end{array}\right];\quad\psi_{1}\left(x\right)\leftrightarrow\vec{e}_{2}=\left[\begin{array}{c} 0\\ 1\\ 0\\ \vdots \end{array}\right]\quad\cdots\quad\psi_{N-1}\left(x\right)\leftrightarrow\vec{e}_{N}=\left[\begin{array}{c} \vdots\\ 0\\ 0\\ 1 \end{array}\right] \end{equation}
  • Thus, we can write the matrix representation of the \(\hat{a}\) operator by finding the individual columns of \(\hat{a}\) in the basis \(\left\{ \psi_{0}\left(x\right),\psi_{1}\left(x\right),\ldots,\psi_{N-1}\left(x\right)\right\} \leftrightarrow\left\{ \vec{e}_{1},\vec{e}_{2},\ldots,\vec{e}_{N}\right\}\)

    • The first column of \(\hat{a}\) in the energy basis is the result of multiplying the \(\hat{a}\) operator in the energy basis by the first elementary vector:

      \begin{equation} \hat{a}\ket{0}=0\leftrightarrow\hat{a}\vec{e}_{1}=\left[\begin{array}{c} 0\\ 0\\ \vdots\\ 0 \end{array}\right] \end{equation}

    • The second column of \(\hat{a}\) in the energy basis is the result of multiplying the \(\hat{a}\) operator in the energy basis by the second elementary vector:

      \begin{equation} \hat{a}\ket{1}=\ket{0}\leftrightarrow\hat{a}\vec{e}_{2}=\vec{e}_{1}=\left[\begin{array}{c} 1\\ 0\\ 0\\ \vdots \end{array}\right] \end{equation}

    • The third column of \(\hat{a}\) in the energy basis is the result of multiplying the \(\hat{a}\) operator in the energy basis by the third elementary vector:

      \begin{equation} \hat{a}\ket{2}=\sqrt{2}\ket{1}\leftrightarrow\hat{a}\vec{e}_{3}=\sqrt{2}\vec{e}_{2}=\left[\begin{array}{c} 0\\ \sqrt{2}\\ 0\\ \vdots \end{array}\right] \end{equation}

    • Then, we can assemble all the columns to obtain the matrix representation of \(\hat{a}\) in the energy basis:

      \begin{equation} \hat{a}\leftrightarrow\left[\begin{array}{ccccc} 0 & 1 & 0 & \cdots & 0\\ 0 & 0 & \sqrt{2} & \cdots & 0\\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \ddots & 0\\ \vdots & \vdots & \vdots & \cdots & \sqrt{N-1}\\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \cdots & 0 \end{array}\right] \end{equation}

      • All elements of \(\hat{a}\) in the energy basis are zero, except the following:

        \begin{equation} a_{n,n+1}=\sqrt{n} \end{equation}

  • We can then easily find the matrix representation of \(\hat{a}^{\dagger}\) in the energy basis:

    \begin{equation} \hat{a}^{\dagger}\leftrightarrow\left[\begin{array}{ccccc} 0 & 0 & 0 & \cdots & 0\\ 1 & 0 & 0 & \cdots & 0\\ 0 & \sqrt{2} & 0 & \ddots & 0\\ \vdots & \vdots & \ddots & \cdots\\ 0 & 0 & 0 & \sqrt{N-1} & 0 \end{array}\right] \end{equation}

  • Next, we can the matrix representation of \(\hat{H}\) in the energy basis:

    \begin{equation} \hat{H}=\hbar\omega\left(\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{I}\right) \end{equation}

Exercise 8.1 Write Python code to form the raising and lowering operators of a quantum Harmonic oscillator with a basis truncated to \(N=6\) states. Use \(m=6\;\text{amu}\) and \(f=300\;\text{inv. cm.}\) Before we get too far, we must discuss units. Einstein's famous equation,

\begin{equation} E=mc^{2}, \end{equation}

tells us that the energy contained in the mass of a particle is proportional to its rest mass, \(m\), by the speed of light squared. One atomic mass unit (amu) is equivalent to 931 MeV. Additionally, a quantum relationship,

\begin{equation} c=f\lambda, \end{equation}

helps us understand frequency in inverse centimeters. This formula states that the product of a photon's frequency \(f\) and its wavelength, \(\lambda\), is \(c\), the speed of light: \(2.997\times10^{8}\) m/s, or \(2.997\times10^{10}\) cm/s. We can rearrange this to be

\begin{equation} f=\frac{c}{\lambda}=\frac{2.997\times10^{10}\;\text{cm/s}}{\lambda\;\text{cm}}. \end{equation}

In quantum or relativistic sciences, it is sometimes customary to use atomic units, where we let physical constants such as \(c\), \(\hbar\), etc., all be unity. Under this convention, frequency takes units of inverse centimeters. We can simply take a frequency given in inverse centimeters and multiply by \(c\) to obtain \(f\) in Hertz:

\begin{equation} f_{\text{Hz}}=cf_{\text{inv. cm.}}. \end{equation}

Given \(f\) in Hz, we can then find angular frequency, \(\omega=2\pi f_{\text{Hz}}\).

Show/hide solution ...

The code given below is one approach to this problem. 


import numpy as np

def dag( Q ):
  '''Calculates the Hermitian adjoint of a quantum object
     (vector or operator).

  Args:
    qobj: an numpy.ndarray representing a operator, bra,
          or ket.

  Returns:
    The Hermitian adjoint of Q.

  '''

  return np.conj( Q.transpose() )

# Constants
hbar = 6.58E-16 # [eV*s] the reduced Planck constant
c_nms = 2.997E17 # [nm/s] speed of light
c_cms = 2.997E10 # [nm/s] speed of light
amu2eV = 931.5E6 # [eV/amu] mass-to-energy conversion
kB = 8.617E-5 # [eV/K]

# Parameters
N = 6
f_invcm = 300 # [inv cm] frequency
m_amu = 6 # [amu] mass

# Calculations
f = f_invcm * c_cms # [Hz] oscillator frequency
T = 1/f # [s] oscillator period
w = 2*np.pi*f # [rad/s] oscillator ang freq

hw = hbar * w # [eV] energy quantization

m = m_amu * amu2eV / c_nms**2 # [eV*s^2/nm^2] mass

Id = np.eye(N) # [] Identity operator

# lowering oeprator
a = np.diag(np.sqrt(range(1,N)), 1)

# raising operator
ad = dag( a )

# Hamiltonian
H = hw*(ad @ a + 0.5*Id)

print('Lowering operator in the energy basis:')
print(a)
print('\nRaising operator in the energy basis:')
print(ad)
print(f'\nOscillator quantization (hbar omega): {hw} eV\n')
print('\nHamiltonian in the energy basis:')
print(H)

 [0.         0.         0.         1.73205081 0.         0.        ]

 [0.         0.         0.         0.         0.         0.20444498]]
  • We can see that \(\hat{H}\) is diagonal in the energy basis
    • You might recall from linear algebra that the eigenvalues of a diagonal matrix are its diagonal elements, and the elementary vectors are the associated eigenvalues.
    • We can immediately read the eigenenergies from the diagonal elements of \(\hat{H}\) in the energy basis
  • The elementary basis vectors may not be incredibly revealing to us, because they represents the eigenstates in the energy basis
  • Thus, it is desirable to transform these states to the position basis
    • To do this, we can:
      • form the position operator in the energy basis using Equation \eqref{org21a42a9}
      • calculate the eigenvectors of the position operator in the energy basis
      • form the eigenvectors into a matrix
        • This matrix transforms position-basis states into energy basis states
      • the Hermitian adjoint of this matrix can be used to transform energy-basis states or operators to the position basis

8.1.2. The Position-Basis Representation for the QHO

Exercise 8.2 Plot probability distributions for the ground state and the first two excited states in both the energy basis and the position basis. Use \(N=15\) as the basis size, \(m=6\) amu, and an oscillator frequency of \(f=300\;\text{inv. cm}\).

Show/hide solution ...

The code given below is one approach to this problem. Our result is given in Figure 36.

Show/hide code ... 

import numpy as np
import os

import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

def dag( Q ):
    '''Calculates the Hermitian adjoint of a quantum object
       (vector or operator).

    Args:
      qobj: an numpy.ndarray representing a operator, bra,
            or ket.

    Returns:
      The Hermitian adjoint of Q.

    '''

    return np.conj( Q.transpose() )

# Constants
hbar = 6.58E-16 # [eV*s] the reduced Planck constant
c_nms = 2.997E17 # [nm/s] speed of light
c_cms = 2.997E10 # [nm/s] speed of light
amu2eV = 931.5E6 # [eV/amu] mass-to-energy conversion
kB = 8.617E-5 # [eV/K]

# Parameters
N = 15
f_invcm = 300 # [inv cm] frequency
m_amu = 6 # [amu] mass

# Calculations
f = f_invcm * c_cms # [Hz] oscillator frequency
T = 1/f # [s] oscillator period
w = 2*np.pi*f # [rad/s] oscillator ang freq

hw = hbar * w # [eV] energy quantization

m = m_amu * amu2eV / c_nms**2 # [eV*s^2/nm^2] mass

Id = np.eye(N) # [] Identity operator

# lowering oeprator
a = np.diag(np.sqrt(range(1,N)), 1)

# raising operator
ad = dag( a )

# Hamiltonian
H = hw*(ad @ a + 0.5*Id)

# Position operator (not the NOT operator)
X = np.sqrt(hbar/(2*m*w)) * (a + ad)

Xo = np.sqrt(hbar/ (m*w)) # Characteristic length

# Eigenvalues of this operator are the possible
# outcomes of a position measurement
# Eigenvectors are returned as a matrix UEX
Xval, UEX = np.linalg.eigh( X ) # [nm, ]

UXE = dag( UEX ) # change basis: energy to position

fig, ax = plt.subplots(3, 2)

for n in range(3):
    # Form the n-th energy eigenstate
    ketnE = np.zeros((N,1), dtype='complex')
    ketnE[n,0] = 1 # assign 1 to the n-th element

    # transform ket_n_E to the position basis
    ketnX = UXE @ ketnE

    # get a probability distribution
    # convert matrices to arrays becayse * triggers a matrix mult,
    # but for arrays, it triggers an element-wise multiplication
    probdistX = np.conj(np.array(ketnX)) * np.array(ketnX)

    probdistE = np.conj(np.array(ketnE)) * np.array(ketnE)

    ax[n,0].barh(np.array(list(range(N))) + 0.5,
                 probdistE.flatten().real)
    ax[n,0].grid(True)
    ax[n,0].set_ylabel(r'$E/\hbar \omega$', fontsize=16)
    ax[n,0].set_xlabel('Probability', fontsize=16)

    ax[n,1].bar(Xval/Xo,
                probdistX.flatten().real)
    ax[n,1].grid(True)
    ax[n,1].set_ylabel('Probability', fontsize=16)
    ax[n,1].set_xlabel(r'$X/X_o$', fontsize=16)

    if n == 0:
        ax[n,0].set_title('Energy Distribution')
        ax[n,1].set_title('Position Distribution')

    plt.tight_layout()

fname = os.path.join('img', 'QHO-ExerciseTransformBasis.png')
plt.savefig( fname )
plt.close('all')

print(f'[[./{fname}]]')

[[./img/QHO-ExerciseTransformBasis.png]]

Figure 36: Probability distributions are plotted for the ground state, first excited state, and second excited state. Each row of subplots shows an eigenstate of \(\hat{H}\). The top row shows the ground state. In the energy basis, only the ground state is occupied. A spatial distribution indicates a probability distribution peaked at the center, \(X=0/X_{o}\). The middle row shows the first excited state, and the bottom row shows the second excited state. Notice that the eigenstates are well-defined in energy space, but distributed over several position values in position space. The code that produced this is given in below.

8.2. Time Dependence of the QHO

  • So far, we looked only at the eigenstates of \(\hat{H}\) for the QHO
  • Let us now consider the time dependence of the QHO

8.2.1. Time Dependence of an Eigenstate of \(\hat{H}\)

  • Consider the time dependence of \(\ket{\psi}\), prepared in an eigenstate state: \(\ket{\psi(0}=\ket{\psi_{n}}\)

    • The time dependence is

      \begin{equation} \begin{aligned} \ket{\psi\left(t\right)} & =e^{-i\hat{H}t/\hbar}\ket{\psi_{n}}\\ & =\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\left(-\frac{it}{\hbar}\right)^{k}\hat{H}^{k}\right)\ket{\psi_{n}}=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\left(-\frac{it}{\hbar}\right)^{k}\hat{H}^{k}\ket{\psi_{n}} \end{aligned} \end{equation}

      • Notice that

        \begin{equation} \begin{aligned} \hat{H}^{k}\ket{\psi_{n}} & =\hat{H}^{k-1}\hat{H}\ket{\psi_{n}}\stackrel{\left(\ref{eq:qho-tise-braket}\right)}{=}\hat{H}^{k-1}E_{n}\ket{\psi_{n}}=E_{n}\hat{H}^{k-1}\ket{\psi_{n}}\\ & =E_{n}\hat{H}^{k-2}\hat{H}\ket{\psi_{n}}=E_{n}^{2}\hat{H}^{k-2}\ket{\psi_{n}}\cdots\\ & =E_{n}^{k}\ket{\psi_{n}} \end{aligned} \end{equation}

      • Thus, our time evolution becomes

        \begin{equation} \label{orgdd7849d} \begin{aligned} \ket{\psi\left(t\right)} & =\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\left(-\frac{it}{\hbar}\right)^{k}E_{n}^{k}\ket{\psi_{n}}=\left(\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{k!}\left(-\frac{it}{\hbar}\right)^{k}E_{n}^{k}\right)\ket{\psi_{n}}\nonumber \\ & =e^{-itE_{n}/\hbar}\ket{\psi_{n}} \end{aligned} \end{equation}
      • We have turned a matrix exponential into a scalar exponential, because the time evolution operator acts on \(\ket{\psi_{n}}\)

  • We can show that the probability distribution for an eigenstate of \(\hat{H}\) is constant. It is easier to do this in the functional form of \(\psi_{n}\left(x,t\right)\)

    \begin{equation} \left|\psi_{n}\left(x,t\right)\right|^{2}=\psi_{n}^{\ast}\left(x,t\right)\psi_{n}\left(x,t\right)=e^{it\hat{H}/\hbar}\psi_{n}^{\ast}\left(x\right)e^{-it\hat{H}/\hbar}\psi_{n}\left(x\right)=\psi_{n}^{\ast}\left(x\right)\psi_{n}\left(x\right)=\left|\psi_{n}\left(x\right)\right|^{2} \end{equation}
    • Thus, the time evolution of the probability distribution \(\left|\psi_{n}\left(x,t\right)\right|^{2}\) for an eigenstate of \(\hat{H}\) is time independent

8.2.2. Time Dependence of non-eigenstates of \(\hat{H}\)

  • We can form noneigenstates of \(\hat{H}\) by making superpositions of eigenstates.

Exercise 8.3 Find the probability distribution of

\begin{equation} \psi\left(x,t\right)=\psi_{m}\left(x\right)e^{-iE_{m}t/\hbar}+\psi_{n}\left(x\right)e^{-iE_{n}t/\hbar}. \end{equation}
Show/hide solution ...

Here, we follow the pattern from before:

\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} \left|\psi\left(x,t\right)\right|^{2} & =\left|\psi_{m}\left(x\right)e^{-iE_{m}t/\hbar}+\psi_{n}\left(x\right)e^{-iE_{n}t/\hbar}\right|^{2}\\ & =\left(\psi_{m}\left(x\right)e^{-iE_{m}t/\hbar}+\psi_{n}\left(x\right)e^{-iE_{n}t/\hbar}\right)^{\ast}\left(\psi_{m}\left(x\right)e^{-iE_{m}t/\hbar}+\psi_{n}\left(x\right)e^{-iE_{n}t/\hbar}\right)\\ & =\left(\psi_{m}^{\ast}\left(x\right)e^{iE_{m}t/\hbar}+\psi_{n}^{\ast}\left(x\right)e^{iE_{n}t/\hbar}\right)\left(\psi_{m}\left(x\right)e^{-iE_{m}t/\hbar}+\psi_{n}\left(x\right)e^{-iE_{n}t/\hbar}\right)\\ & =\psi_{m}^{\ast}\left(x\right)\psi_{m}\left(x\right)+\psi_{m}^{\ast}\left(x\right)\psi_{n}\left(x\right)e^{-i\left(E_{n}-E_{m}\right)t/\hbar}+\psi_{m}\left(x\right)\psi_{n}^{\ast}\left(x\right)e^{-i\left(E_{m}-E_{n}\right)t/\hbar}+\psi_{n}^{\ast}\left(x\right)\psi_{n}\left(x\right)\\ & =\left|\psi_{m}\right|^{2}+\psi_{m}^{\ast}\left(x\right)\psi_{n}\left(x\right)e^{-i\left(E_{n}-E_{m}\right)t/\hbar}+\psi_{m}\left(x\right)\psi_{n}^{\ast}\left(x\right)e^{-i\left(E_{m}-E_{n}\right)t/\hbar}+\left|\psi_{n}\right|^{2} \end{aligned} \end{equation}

Notice that the superposition of two eigenstates now has a time-dependent probability distribution.

8.2.3. Time Dependence of Various initial Conditions

Exercise 8.4 Find the probability distribution of a system prepared in the initial state

\begin{equation} \psi\left(x,0\right)=\sum_{n=0}^{N}c_{n}\psi_{n}\left(x\right). \end{equation}
Show/hide solution ...

Apply the time evolution operator:

\begin{equation} \begin{aligned} \psi\left(x,t\right) & =e^{-it\hat{H}/\hbar}\psi\left(x,0\right)=e^{-it\hat{H}/\hbar}\sum_{n=0}^{N}c_{n}\psi_{n}\left(x\right)\\ & =\sum_{n=0}^{N}c_{n}e^{-it\hat{H}/\hbar}\psi_{n}\left(x\right)\\ & \stackrel{\left(\ref{eq:EigenstateEvolution}\right)}{=}\sum_{n=0}^{N}c_{n}e^{-itE_{n}/\hbar}\psi_{n}\left(x\right) \end{aligned} \end{equation}

Exercise 8.5 Plot the time-dependent probability distributions over position and energy for a quantum harmonic oscillator for three different initial states:

\begin{equation} \label{orge7ff35e} \psi_{a}\left(x,0\right)=\psi_{2}\left(x\right) \end{equation} \begin{equation} \label{org373f3a9} \psi_{b}\left(x,0\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\psi_{1}\left(x\right)+\psi_{2}\left(x\right)\right),\;\text{and} \end{equation} \begin{equation} \label{org32c10cb} \psi_{c}\left(x,0\right)=D\left(\alpha\right)\psi_{0}\left(x\right), \end{equation}

where \(D\left(\alpha\right)\) is the displacement operator,

\begin{equation} D\left(\alpha\right)=e^{\alpha\hat{a}^{\dagger}-\alpha\hat{a}} \end{equation}

for a complex parameter \(\alpha\). You should see that the state \(\psi_{c}\left(x,0\right)\) is a coherent state or "Glauber state," and it behaves like a classical oscillator.

Show/hide solution ...

The instructor solution is given below, with results plotted in Figure 37.

Show/hide code ... 

import numpy as np
import os
from scipy.linalg import expm

import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

def dag( Q ):
    '''Calculates the Hermitian adjoint of a quantum object
       (vector or operator).

    Args:
      qobj: an numpy.ndarray representing a operator, bra,
            or ket.

    Returns:
      The Hermitian adjoint of Q.

    '''

    return np.conj( Q.transpose() )

def elementary_vector( n:int , d:int ):
    """
       Returns the n-th elementary vector in a
       d-dimensional vector space

       Args
         n: index of elementary vector

         d: dimension of elementary vector

       Returns an np.ndarray()
    """

    v = np.zeros([d, 1], dtype='complex');
    v[n, 0] = 1

    return v

def displacement_operator( alpha ):
    """
       Returns the displacement operator for the
       quantum harmonic oscillator

       Args
         alpha: complex displacement parameter

       Returns an NxN np.ndarray()
    """

    return expm( alpha*a - alpha*ad )

def state_to_probability( psi, verbose=False):
    """
       Convert a state vector to a probability
       distribution.

       Args
         psi: a state vector

       Returns an (N,) np.ndarray()
    """

    temp = np.conj(np.array(psi)) * np.array(psi)

    return temp.flatten().real

def expectation_value( op, psi ):
    """
       Return the expectation value for an
       operator op.

       Args
         psi: a state vector

       Returns real expectation value
    """

    temp = dag( psi ) @ op @ psi

    return temp[0,0].real

# Constants
hbar = 6.58E-16 # [eV*s] the reduced Planck constant
c_nms = 2.997E17 # [nm/s] speed of light
c_cms = 2.997E10 # [nm/s] speed of light
amu2eV = 931.5E6 # [eV/amu] mass-to-energy conversion
kB = 8.617E-5 # [eV/K]

# Parameters
N = 15
f_invcm = 300 # [inv cm] frequency
m_amu = 6 # [amu] mass

# Calculations
f = f_invcm * c_cms # [Hz] oscillator frequency
T = 1/f # [s] oscillator period

nt=75 # number of time points
t = np.linspace(0, 5*T, nt) # time vector

w = 2*np.pi*f # [rad/s] oscillator ang freq

hw = hbar * w # [eV] energy quantization

m = m_amu * amu2eV / c_nms**2 # [eV*s^2/nm^2] mass

Id = np.eye(N) # [] Identity operator

# lowering oeprator
a = np.diag(np.sqrt(range(1,N)), 1)

# raising operator
ad = dag( a )

# Hamiltonian
H = hw*(ad @ a + 0.5*Id) # [eV]

Eval, Psin = np.linalg.eigh( H ) # [eV, ]

# Position operator (not the NOT operator)
X = np.sqrt(hbar/(2*m*w)) * (a + ad)

Xo = np.sqrt(hbar/ (m*w)) # Characteristic length

# Eigenvalues of this operator are the possible
# outcomes of a position measurement
# Eigenvectors are returned as a matrix UEX
Xval, UEX = np.linalg.eigh( X ) # [nm, ]

UXE = dag( UEX ) # change basis: energy to position

fig, ax = plt.subplots(3, 2) # array of six plots

# Establish three initital states
# Form the n-th energy eigenstate
psi0a = elementary_vector(2, N)

psi0b = (1/np.sqrt(2))*( elementary_vector( 2, N ) \
                         + elementary_vector( 3, N ) )

psi0c = displacement_operator( 1 ) @  elementary_vector( 0, N )

# Wrap the three intial states in a vector so we can iterate
psi0 = [psi0a, psi0b, psi0c]

# Storage vector for energy, position distribution
pdistE = [np.zeros( (N, nt) )] * 3
pdistX = [np.zeros( (N, nt) )] * 3

Et = np.zeros((3, nt))
pos_exp = np.zeros((3, nt))

# meshes for 2D plotting
tt, xx = np.meshgrid(t.flatten()/T, Xval/Xo, indexing='xy')
_, EE = np.meshgrid(t.flatten()/T, Eval/hw, indexing='xy')

fig, ax = plt.subplots( 3, 2 )

# Loop over each initial condition
for ic_idx in range(3):

    # Loop over each time step
    for it, tv in enumerate(t):

        # time-dependent state
        psit = expm( -1j*tv*H/hbar) @ psi0[ic_idx]

        # probability distribution over energy
        pdistE[ic_idx][:,it] = state_to_probability( psit ).flatten()

        # probability distribution over position
        pdistX[ic_idx][:,it] = state_to_probability( UXE @ psit ).flatten()

        # energy expectation value [eV]
        Et[ic_idx, it] = expectation_value( H, psit )

        # position exepcation value [nm]
        pos_exp[ic_idx, it] = expectation_value( X, psit )

    ax[ic_idx,0].contourf(tt, EE,pdistE[ic_idx], levels = 75)
    ax[ic_idx,0].plot(t/T, Et[ic_idx]/hw, color='w')
    ax[ic_idx,0].set_ylabel(r'$E/\hbar \omega$', fontsize=16)

    ax[ic_idx,1].contourf(tt, xx, pdistX[ic_idx], levels=75)
    ax[ic_idx,1].plot(t/T, pos_exp[ic_idx]/Xo, color='w')
    ax[ic_idx,1].set_ylabel(r'$X/X_o$', fontsize=16)

    if ic_idx == 2:
        ax[ic_idx,0].set_xlabel(r'$t/T$', fontsize=16)
        ax[ic_idx,1].set_xlabel(r'$t/T$', fontsize=16)

    plt.tight_layout()

figname = os.path.join("img", "qho_evolution_vari_ic.png")
plt.savefig(figname)

print(f'\n[[./{figname}]]')


[[./img/qho_evolution_vari_ic.png]]

Figure 37: Probability distributions are plotted for the ground states defined in Equations \eqref{orge7ff35e}-\eqref{org32c10cb}. The top row is for \(\psi_{a}\left(x,0\right)=\psi_{n}\left(x\right)\) ; the middle row is for a linear combination of eigenstates; and the bottom row is for a Glauber state as the initial condition. The expectation value of energy or position is plotted in white. The code that produced this is given in the code above.

  • Notice that in all cases, the expectation value of energy is constant
    • These systems are closed quantum systems, where energy cannot enter or exit
    • Energy is conserved
  • To get to dissipative models that allow energy to escape into the environment, we take two steps:
    • First, move from state-vector formalism with the Schrödinger equation to the density matrix formalism with the quantum Liouville equation
    • Extend the quantum Liouville equation to the Lindblad equation

8.3. Time Dependence of the QHO using the Quantum Liouville Equation

  • We calculate the time-dependence of the QHO using density operator formalism
    • We use some code tools I developed to model QHOs

  • Code that does this calculation is provided below. Results are shown in Figures 38 and 39 

    
import numpy as np
import scipy as sp
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import os
import odeintw

# Now we import from a custom module I wrote
# These are found in the directory "../org/PyQCA/"
homedir = os.getcwd() # bookmark this working directory
from qcapackage.quantum.harmonicoscillator import HarmonicOscillator as QHO
from qcapackage.quantum import linearalgebra as la
from qcapackage import constants as const

from qcapackage.quantum.timeevolution import quantum_liouville

# select the initial state
start = 'coherent'

N = 15
nt = 75

# Create a QHO object
# Uses default m = 6 amu and f = 300 inv cm
# This also creates all the operators:
#   Hamiltonian, raising and lowering,
#   position, momentum
# It also calculates the characteristic
#   length and energy
qosc = QHO(N=N)

X0 = qosc.Q0 # [nm] characteristic length

T = qosc.T # oscillation period
t = np.linspace(0, 3*T, nt).flatten()

# Storage arrays
PDE = np.zeros([N, nt]) # prob dist over energy
PDX = np.zeros([N, nt]) # prob dist over position
Et = np.zeros((nt,))
Xt = np.zeros((nt,))

# Prepare the initial state
if start == 'eigenstate':
    psi0 = qosc.energy_state(0)

elif start == 'coherent':
    d = 1 + 1j # displacement
    D = qosc.displacement_operator(d)
    # Recall that D|0> "selects" the first column of D
    psi0 = D @ qosc.energy_state(0)

elif start == 'arbitrary':
    psi0 = la.normalize( qosc.energy_state(1) + qosc.energy_state(2) )

# Exponential time evolution (state-vector formalism)
for it, tt in enumerate(t):
    Ut = sp.linalg.expm(-1j * qosc.H * tt / const.hbar )

    psit = Ut @ psi0
    psitX = qosc.change_basis(psit, fr='energy', to='position')

    PDE[:,it] = la.state_to_probability(psit).real
    PDX[:,it] = la.state_to_probability(psitX).real
    Et[it] = la.expectation_value( qosc.H, psit, validate=False )
    Xt[it] = la.expectation_value( qosc.X, psit, validate=False )

fig, ax = plt.subplots( 2, 1 )

tt, xx = np.meshgrid(t.flatten()/qosc.T, qosc.Xval, indexing='xy')
_, EE = np.meshgrid(t.flatten()/T, qosc.Eval/qosc.hw, indexing='xy')

ax[0].contourf(tt, EE, PDE, levels = 75)
ax[0].set_ylabel(r'$E/\hbar \omega$', fontsize=16)

ax[1].contourf(tt, xx, PDX, levels=75)
ax[1].set_ylabel(r'$x$', fontsize=16)

figname = os.path.join("img",
                       "qho_evolution_unitary_PD.png")
plt.savefig(figname)

print(f'\n[[./{figname}]]')

# Quantum Liouville evolution
from odeintw import odeintw

rho0 = la.density_matrix(psi0) # form a density operator

# Integrate the solution
sol = odeintw(quantum_liouville, rho0, t, args=(qosc.H,) )

PDE_ode = np.zeros([N, nt])
PDX_ode = np.zeros([N, nt])
Et_ode = np.zeros((nt,))
Xt_ode = np.zeros((nt,))

for it in range(nt):
    PDE_ode[:,it] = np.diag(sol[it]).real
    rhoX = qosc.change_basis(sol[it], fr='energy', to='position')
    PDX_ode[:,it] = np.diag(rhoX).real
    Et_ode[it] = la.expectation_value( qosc.H, sol[it], validate=False )
    Xt_ode[it] = la.expectation_value( qosc.X, sol[it], validate=False )

# Postprocessing
print(type(sol))
print(sol.shape)

print(sol[0].shape)

fig, ax = plt.subplots( 2, 1 )

ax[0].contourf(tt, xx/qosc.Q0, PDX, levels = 75)
ax[0].plot(t/T, Xt/X0, color='w')
ax[0].set_ylabel(r'$X/X_0$ (expm)', fontsize=16)

ax[1].contourf(tt, xx/X0, PDX_ode, levels=75)
ax[1].plot(t/T, Xt_ode/X0, color='w')
ax[1].set_ylabel(r'$X/X_0$ (ODE)', fontsize=16)
ax[1].set_xlabel(r'$t/T$', fontsize=16)

figname = os.path.join("img", "qho_class_test_evolution_unitary_vs_ODE_XPD.png")
plt.savefig(figname)

print(f'\n[[./{figname}]]')

plt.close('all')

fig, ax = plt.subplots( 2, 1 )

ax[0].contourf(tt, EE, PDE, levels = 75)
ax[0].plot(t/T, Et/qosc.hw, color='w')
ax[0].set_ylabel(r'$E/\hbar \omega$ (expm)', fontsize=16)

ax[1].contourf(tt, EE, PDE_ode, levels=75)
ax[1].plot(t/T, Et_ode/qosc.hw, color='w')
ax[1].set_ylabel(r'$E/\hbar \omega$ (ODE)', fontsize=16)

figname = os.path.join("img", "qho_class_test_evolution_unitary_vs_ODE_EPD.png")
plt.savefig(figname)

print(f'\n[[./{figname}]]')

plt.close('all')

    • Figure 38 shows that the time dynamics according to the Schrödinger equation match those of the quantum Liouville equation

    • These dynamics describe a closed system, where the energy is conserved within the system, as shown in Figure 39

      Figure 38: Dynamics calculated using the quantum Liouville equation match those calculated using the Schrödinger equation. This result is shown in the position basis.

      Figure 39: Dynamics calculated using the quantum Liouville equation match those calculated using the Schrödinger equation. This result is shown in the energy basis.

8.4. Open System Dynamics using the Lindblad Equation

  • One way to treat dissipation in a quantum system is to use the Lindblad equation

    \begin{equation} \label{org92b9b46} \frac{d\hat{\rho}}{dt}=-\frac{i}{\hbar}\left[\hat{H},\hat{\rho}\right]+\sum_{j}\hat{L}_{j}\hat{\rho}\hat{L}_{j}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{ \hat{L}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{j},\hat{\rho}\right\} , \end{equation}

    where the operators \(\left\{ \hat{L}_{j}\right\}\) are called Lindblad operators or dissipation channels

    • As before, \(\hat{H}\) is the Hamiltonian and \(\hat{\rho}\) is the density operator
    • This is a matrix differential equation, like the quantum Liouville equation [see Equation \eqref{orgedc5496}].
    • The Lindblad equation contains a term called the Liouvillian, which is the right-hand side of Equation \eqref{orgedc5496}
      • This part is said to be the unitary part or the Liouvillian part
      • It is responsible for the unitary (closed-system) dynamics of a quantum state

    • Equation \eqref{org92b9b46} also has an additional term, called the Lindbladian or the dissipator:

      \begin{equation} \label{org86a4819} \mathcal{D}=\sum_{j}\hat{L}_{j}\hat{\rho}\hat{L}_{j}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{ \hat{L}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{j},\hat{\rho}\right\} \end{equation}
      • The Lindbladian is responsible for the non-unitary dynamics of a model
        • The effects of the environment on the system are contained within the Lindblad operators
      • A specific choice of the Lindblad operators is a specific model for the environment

8.4.1. Lindblad Operators for the Quantum Harmonic Oscillator - Dissipation (Relaxation)

  • For the quantum harmonic oscillator, we can use a single Lindblad operator,

    \begin{equation} \left\{ \hat{L}_{1}\right\} =\left\{ \frac{1}{\sqrt{T_{1}}}\hat{a}\right\} \end{equation}
    • This causes an exponential decay in system energy with time constant \(T_{1}\)
      • \(T_{1}\) is a measure of system-environment coupling:
        • A short \(T_{1}\) allows a fast exponential decay, meaning the environment and our quantum system are strongly coupled
    • Energy decay means that the system can lose energy
      • Thus, the system is said to be an open system
    • The loss of energy is also called relaxation, since the system relaxes from a high-energy state toward the ground state
      • The loss of energy is also called energy dissipation
    • After applying this operator for a long time, we should expect to find the system in its ground state
  • The code below calculates the time evolution of a QHO under relaxation, and results are shown in Figures 40 (position basis) and 41 (energy basis)
Show/hide code ... 

import numpy as np
import scipy as sp
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import os
import odeintw
import datetime as dt

from qcapackage.quantum.harmonicoscillator import HarmonicOscillator as QHO
from qcapackage.quantum import linearalgebra as la
from qcapackage import constants as const

from qcapackage.quantum.timeevolution import quantum_liouville, lindblad

# select the initial state
start = 'coherent'

N = 15
nt = 500

# Create a QHO object
# Uses default m = 6 amu and f = 300 inv cm
qosc = QHO(N=N)

X0 = qosc.Q0 # [nm] characteristic length

T = qosc.T # [s] oscillation period

# Form Lindblad operators
#   The relaxation time is chosen to be 2*T
qosc.make_lindblad_operators( 2*T, type='relaxation' )

t = np.linspace(0, 8*T, nt).flatten()

# Storage arrays
PDE = np.zeros([N, nt]) # prob dist over energy
PDX = np.zeros([N, nt]) # prob dist over position
Et = np.zeros((nt,))
Xt = np.zeros((nt,))

# Prepare the initial state
if start == 'eigenstate':
  psi0 = qosc.energy_state(0)

elif start == 'coherent':
  d = 1 + 1j # displacement
  D = qosc.displacement_operator(d)
  # Recall that D|0> "selects" the first column of D
  psi0 = D @ qosc.energy_state(0)

elif start == 'arbitrary':
  psi0 = la.normalize( qosc.energy_state(1) + qosc.energy_state(2) )

# Exponential time evolution
for it, tt in enumerate(t):
  Ut = sp.linalg.expm(-1j * qosc.H * tt / const.hbar )

  psit = Ut @ psi0
  psitX = qosc.change_basis(psit, fr='energy', to='position')

  PDE[:,it] = la.state_to_probability(psit).real
  PDX[:,it] = la.state_to_probability(psitX).real
  Et[it] = la.expectation_value( qosc.H, psit, validate=False )
  Xt[it] = la.expectation_value( qosc.X, psit, validate=False )

fig, ax = plt.subplots( 2, 1 )

tt, xx = np.meshgrid(t.flatten()/qosc.T, qosc.Xval, indexing='xy')
_, EE = np.meshgrid(t.flatten()/T, qosc.Eval/qosc.hw, indexing='xy')

figname = os.path.join("img", "qho_class_test_evolution_unitary_PD.png")
plt.savefig(figname)

print(f'\n[[./{figname}]]')

# Quantum Liouville evolution
from odeintw import odeintw

rho0 = la.density_matrix(psi0) # form a density operator

# Integrate the solution
# odeintw produces an array sol
#   Each element of sol is the density matrix at a
#   particular time
# Extract expectation values after calling odeintw
sol = odeintw(quantum_liouville, rho0, t, args=(qosc.H,) )

PDE_liou = np.zeros([N, nt])
PDX_liou = np.zeros([N, nt])
Et_liou = np.zeros((nt,))
Xt_liou = np.zeros((nt,))

for it in range(nt):
  PDE_liou[:,it] = np.diag(sol[it]).real
  rhoX = qosc.change_basis(sol[it], fr='energy', to='position')
  PDX_liou[:,it] = np.diag(rhoX).real
  Et_liou[it] = la.expectation_value( qosc.H, sol[it], validate=False )
  Xt_liou[it] = la.expectation_value( qosc.X, sol[it], validate=False )

# Postprocessing
print(type(sol))
print(sol.shape)

# Lindblad evolution
sol = odeintw(lindblad, rho0, t,
   args=(qosc.H, qosc.LindbladOps))

PDE_lind = np.zeros([N, nt])
PDX_lind = np.zeros([N, nt])
Et_lind = np.zeros((nt,))
Xt_lind = np.zeros((nt,))

for it in range(nt):
  PDE_lind[:,it] = np.diag(sol[it]).real
  rhoX = qosc.change_basis(sol[it], fr='energy', to='position')
  PDX_lind[:,it] = np.diag(rhoX).real
  Et_lind[it] = la.expectation_value( qosc.H, sol[it], validate=False )
  Xt_lind[it] = la.expectation_value( qosc.X, sol[it], validate=False)

fig, ax = plt.subplots( 2, 1 )

ax[0].contourf(tt, xx/qosc.Q0, PDX_liou, levels = 75)
ax[0].plot(t/T, Xt_liou/X0, color='w')
ax[0].set_ylabel(r'$X/X_0$ (Closed)', fontsize=16)

ax[1].contourf(tt, xx/X0, PDX_lind, levels=75)
ax[1].plot(t/T, Xt_lind/X0, color='w')
ax[1].set_ylabel(r'$X/X_0$ (Open)', fontsize=16)
ax[1].set_xlabel(r'$t/T$', fontsize=16)

plt.tight_layout()

figname = os.path.join("img", "qho_unitary_vs_lind_XPD.png")
plt.savefig(figname)

print(f'\n[[./{figname}]]')

plt.close('all')

fig, ax = plt.subplots( 2, 1 )

ax[0].contourf(tt, EE, PDE_liou, levels=75)
ax[0].plot(t/T, Et_liou/qosc.hw, color='w')
ax[0].set_ylim(0.5, 8)
ax[0].set_ylabel(r'$E/\hbar \omega$ (Closed)', fontsize=16)

ax[1].contourf(tt, EE, PDE_lind, levels=75)
ax[1].plot(t/T, Et_lind/qosc.hw, color='w')
ax[1].set_ylim(0.5, 8)
ax[1].set_ylabel(r'$E/\hbar \omega$ (Open)', fontsize=16)
ax[1].set_xlabel(r'$t/T$', fontsize=16)

plt.tight_layout()

figname = os.path.join("img", "qho_unitary_vs_lind_EPD.png")
plt.savefig(figname)

print(f'\n[[./{figname}]]')

plt.close('all')

print(f'\nCode executed at {dt.datetime.now()}')

8.4.2. Lindblad Operators for the Quantum Harmonic Oscillator - Thermalization

  • Another option for the QHO is to use a pair of Lindblad operators,

    \begin{equation} \left\{ \hat{L}_{1},\hat{L}_{2}\right\} =\left\{ \frac{1}{\sqrt{T_{1}}}\hat{a},\frac{1}{\sqrt{T_{1}}}e^{-\frac{\hbar\omega}{k_{B}T}}\hat{a}^{\dagger}\right\} \end{equation}
    • Here, the second Lindblad operator is proportional to the raising operator, \(\hat{a}^{\dagger}\), so we should expect it to excite the system (adding energy)
      • The second Lindblad operator, \(\hat{L}_{2}\), has an additional (small) weight of \(e^{-\frac{\hbar\omega}{2k_{B}T}}\)
        • This maintains relaxation via \(\hat{L}_{1}\) as the dominant effect, but models the fact that the system can be excited by thermal energy in the environment at temperature \(T\)
      • In the limit of \(T\rightarrow0\), the operator \(\hat{L}_{2}\rightarrow0\), so that we have relaxation, as in the previous case
  • The code below calculates the time evolution of a QHO under thermalization, and results are shown in Figures 40 (position basis) and 41 (energy basis)
Show/hide code ...

import scipy as sp
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt

8.4.3. Lindblad Evolution: steady-state Behaviors

  • It may not be evident how the dynamics of dissipation (relaxation) differ from thermalization
    • After all, Figures 40 and 41 are very similar to Figures 42 and 43.
  • It may be more useful to compare steady-state behaviors
    • This is the result of applying the Lindblad operators for a long time, so that \(d\rho/dt\rightarrow0\), that is, we get an unchanging \(\hat{\rho}\left(t\right)\)

    • Thus, we would like to solve for the steady-state \(\rho_{\text{ss}}\) that solves

      \begin{equation} \frac{d\rho}{dt}=-\frac{i}{\hbar}\left[\hat{H},\hat{\rho}\right]+\sum_{j}\hat{L}_{j}\hat{\rho}\hat{L}_{j}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{ \hat{L}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{j},\hat{\rho}\right\} =0 \end{equation}
  • The Python qutip package can calculate Lindblad dynamics, as well as steady-state density matrices
  • Some notes on using qutip to solve the Lindblad equation
    • qutip provides a class, qutip.Qobj, which is used to describe quantum objects such as kets and operators
    • Use the mesolve() function to describe and solve a Lindblad evolution in time
      • mesolve is short for "master equation solve," because the Lindblad equation is known as a master equation

      • The syntax for using mesolve is 

        result = mesolve(
    H,    # qutip.Qobj, Hamiltonian operator
    rho0, # qutip.Qobj, initial density operator
    t,    # numpy time vector for the evolution
    c_ops=c_ops, # list of Lindblad operators (collapse operators, qutip.Qobj)
    e_ops=e_ops # list of measurement operators (expectation values desired, qutip.Qobj)
)

      • mesolve uses natural units, where \(\hbar=1\):

        \begin{equation} \frac{d\rho}{dt}=-i\left[\hat{H},\hat{\rho}\right]+\sum_{j}\hat{L}_{j}\hat{\rho}\hat{L}_{j}^{\dagger}-\frac{1}{2}\left\{ \hat{L}_{j}^{\dagger}\hat{L}_{j},\hat{\rho}\right\} =0 \end{equation}
        • Here, \(\hat{H}\) has units of not eV, but rather eV/\(\hbar\). Thus, instead of using \(\hat{H}=\hbar\omega\left(\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}+\hat{1}/2\right)\) for the Hamiltonian, we use \(\hat{H}=\omega\left(\hat{a}^{\dagger}\hat{a}+\hat{1}/2\right)\)

    • Use the steadystate(H, c_ops…)= function to calculate the steady-state density matrix given a Hamiltonian stored in H a list of Lindblad operators specified using the keyword argument c_ops 

      
import numpy as np
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import os
import datetime as dt

from qutip import (
    basis,
    destroy,
    displace,
    mesolve,
    steadystate,
    Qobj,
)

def estimate_temperature_from_populations(p, hw, kB):
    """
    Estimate temperature assuming

        p_n ~ exp[- hw*n/(kT)]

    Returns:
        Teff
        slope
        intercept
    """

    n = np.arange(len(p))

    mask = p > 1e-12

    coeff = np.polyfit(
        n[mask],
        np.log(p[mask]),
        1
    )

    slope = coeff[0]
    intercept = coeff[1]

    Teff = -hw / (kB * slope)

    return Teff, slope, intercept

# ============================================================
# Physical constants
# ============================================================

hbar_ev_s = 6.582119569e-16      # eV*s
kB = 8.617333262145e-5           # eV/K
c_cms = 2.99792458e10            # cm/s

# SI constants
hbar_si = 1.054571817e-34        # J*s
amu_kg = 1.66053906660e-27       # kg

# ============================================================
# Parameters
# ============================================================

start = "coherent"       # "eigenstate", "coherent", "arbitrary"

N = 15
nt = 120

m_amu = 6
f_invcm = 300
Temp = 300

T1_factor = 2.0

# ============================================================
# Harmonic oscillator
# ============================================================

f = f_invcm * c_cms  # [Hz] oscillation frequency
Tosc = 1.0 / f       # [s] oscillation period

w = 2.0 * np.pi * f  # [rad/s] oscillation angular frequency

hw = hbar_ev_s * w   # [eV] energy quantization

m = m_amu * amu_kg   # [kg] oscillator mass

X0 = np.sqrt(hbar_si / (m * w)) # [nm] characteristic length

# ============================================================
# Operators
# ============================================================

a = destroy(N)
adag = a.dag()

H = hw * (adag * a + 0.5) # [eV]

X = np.sqrt(hbar_si / (2 * m * w)) * (a + adag)

# ============================================================
# Initial state
# ============================================================

if start == "eigenstate":

    psi0 = basis(N, 0)

elif start == "coherent":

    alpha = 1 + 1j
    psi0 = displace(N, alpha) * basis(N, 0)

elif start == "arbitrary":

    psi0 = (basis(N, 1) + basis(N, 2)).unit()

else:

    raise ValueError(f"Unknown start = {start}")

rho0 = psi0.proj()

# ============================================================
# Time grid
# ============================================================

t = np.linspace(0.0, 8.0 * Tosc, nt)

# ============================================================
# Position basis
# ============================================================

Xmat = X.full()

xvals, U = np.linalg.eigh(Xmat)

# ============================================================
# Projectors onto energy eigenstates
# ============================================================

energy_projectors = [
    basis(N, n).proj()
    for n in range(N)
]

# ============================================================
# Projectors onto position eigenstates
# ============================================================

position_projectors = []

for i in range(N):

    xket = Qobj(
        U[:, i].reshape((N, 1)),
        dims=[[N], [1]]
    )

    position_projectors.append(
        xket.proj()
    )

# ============================================================
# e_ops
# ============================================================

e_ops = (
    [H, X]
    + energy_projectors
    + position_projectors
)

# ============================================================
# Closed-system evolution
# ============================================================

result_unitary = mesolve(
    H/hbar_ev_s,
    rho0,
    t,
    c_ops=[],
    e_ops=e_ops
)

# ============================================================
# Open-system evolution
# ============================================================

T1 = T1_factor * Tosc

gamma_down = 1.0 / T1

gamma_up = (
    np.exp(-hw / (kB * Temp))
    / T1
)

c_ops = [
    np.sqrt(gamma_down) * a,
    np.sqrt(gamma_up) * adag,
]

result_open = mesolve(
    H/hbar_ev_s,
    rho0,
    t,
    c_ops=c_ops,
    e_ops=e_ops
)

# ============================================================
# Expectation values
# ============================================================

E_unit = np.asarray(result_unitary.expect[0])
X_unit = np.asarray(result_unitary.expect[1])

E_open = np.asarray(result_open.expect[0])
X_open = np.asarray(result_open.expect[1])

# ============================================================
# Probability distributions
# ============================================================

PDE_unit = np.zeros((N, nt))
PDE_open = np.zeros((N, nt))

PDX_unit = np.zeros((N, nt))
PDX_open = np.zeros((N, nt))

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Energy probabilities
# ------------------------------------------------------------

for n in range(N):

    PDE_unit[n, :] = np.real(
        result_unitary.expect[2 + n]
    )

    PDE_open[n, :] = np.real(
        result_open.expect[2 + n]
    )

# ------------------------------------------------------------
# Position probabilities
# ------------------------------------------------------------

offset = 2 + N

for n in range(N):

    PDX_unit[n, :] = np.real(
        result_unitary.expect[offset + n]
    )

    PDX_open[n, :] = np.real(
        result_open.expect[offset + n]
    )

# ============================================================
# Steady state density matrices
# ============================================================
rho_ss_relx = steadystate(H/hbar_ev_s, c_ops[:1]) # relaxation/dissipation
rho_ss_therm = steadystate(H/hbar_ev_s, c_ops) # thermalization

# ============================================================
# Thermal density matrix
# ============================================================

beta = 1.0 / (kB * Temp)

thermal_weights = np.exp(
    -beta * H.eigenenergies()
)

thermal_weights /= np.sum(thermal_weights)

rho_thermal = Qobj(
    np.diag(thermal_weights)
)

# ============================================================
# Steady-state distributions
# ============================================================
P_energy_ss_relx = np.real(
    np.diag(rho_ss_relx.full())
)
P_energy_ss_therm = np.real(
    np.diag(rho_ss_therm.full())
)

# Convert density matrix to position basis
rho_x_ss_relx = U.conj().T @ rho_ss_relx.full() @ U
rho_x_ss_therm = U.conj().T @ rho_ss_therm.full() @ U

P_position_ss_relx = np.real(
    np.diag(rho_x_ss_relx)
)
P_position_ss_therm = np.real(
    np.diag(rho_x_ss_therm)
)

# ============================================================
# Thermal-state distributions
# ============================================================

P_energy_thermal = np.real(
    np.diag(rho_thermal.full())
)

rho_x_thermal = (
    U.conj().T
    @ rho_thermal.full()
    @ U
)

P_position_thermal = np.real(
    np.diag(rho_x_thermal)
)

# ============================================================
# Compare thermal steady state with thermal density matrix
# ============================================================

rho_ss_mat = rho_ss_therm.full()
rho_th_mat = rho_thermal.full()

# Frobenius norm

frobenius_error = np.linalg.norm(
    rho_ss_mat - rho_th_mat
)

# Trace distance
trace_distance = (
    0.5
    * np.sum(
        np.abs(
            np.linalg.eigvals(
                rho_ss_mat - rho_th_mat
            )
        )
    )
)

# Energy expectation values

E_ss = np.real(
    (rho_ss_mat @ H.full()).trace()
)

E_th = np.real(
    (rho_th_mat @ H.full()).trace()
)

print()
print("Comparison with thermal density matrix")
print("--------------------------------------")
print(f"Frobenius error : {frobenius_error:.6e}")
print(f"Trace distance  : {trace_distance:.6e}")
print(f"E_ss            : {E_ss/hw:.6f} ")
print(f"E_thermal       : {E_th/hw:.6f} ")
print()

print(
    "||rho_ss - rho_th||_F =",
    np.linalg.norm(
        rho_ss_therm.full() -
        rho_thermal.full()
    )
)

# ============================================================
# Temperature fitting for thermal states
# ============================================================

Tfit_thermal, slope_th, b_th = (
    estimate_temperature_from_populations(
        P_energy_thermal,
        hw,
        kB
    )
)

Tfit_ss, slope_ss, b_ss = (
    estimate_temperature_from_populations(
        P_energy_ss_therm,
        hw,
        kB
    )
)

print()
print("Boltzmann Temperatures")
print("----------------------")
print(f"Input temperature       : {Temp:.3f} K")
print(f"Thermal density matrix  : {Tfit_thermal:.3f} K")
print(f"Lindblad steady state   : {Tfit_ss:.3f} K")
print()

# ============================================================
# Energy axis
# ============================================================

energy_levels = H.eigenenergies() / hw

# ============================================================
# Position dynamics
# ============================================================

fig, ax = plt.subplots(
    2,
    1,
    figsize=(9, 8),
    sharex=True
)

ax[0].pcolormesh(
    t / Tosc,
    xvals / X0,
    PDX_unit,
    shading="auto",
    cmap="viridis"
)

ax[0].plot(
    t / Tosc,
    X_unit / X0,
    color="white",
    linewidth=2
)

ax[0].grid(True, alpha=0.2)

ax[0].set_ylabel(r"$X/X_0$ (Closed)")
ax[0].set_title("Position Probability Distribution")

ax[1].pcolormesh(
    t / Tosc,
    xvals / X0,
    PDX_open,
    shading="auto",
    cmap="viridis"
)

ax[1].plot(
    t / Tosc,
    X_open / X0,
    color="white",
    linewidth=2
)

ax[1].grid(True, alpha=0.2)

ax[1].set_ylabel(r"$X/X_0$ (Open)")
ax[1].set_xlabel(r"$t/T$")

fig.tight_layout()

figname = os.path.join("img", "qho_qutip-closed-vs-thermalization-position-dynamics.png")
plt.savefig(figname)
plt.close("all")

9. Molecular Vibrations in QCA

  • When an electron occupies a molecular quantum dot, the bond lengths to nearby atoms can change
  • When an electron transfers from one dot to another, these changes in atomic bonds result in molecular vibrations

9.1. Hamiltonian for a QCA Cell with Molecular Vibrations

  • To model molecular vibrations, we can use a quantum harmonic oscillator
  • To associate these vibrations with electron transfer events in a QCA cell, we can make a composite quantum system of a two-state QCA cell and the quantum harmonic oscillator

    • The Hamiltonian for this system may be written as

      \begin{equation} \label{org91c05dd} \hat{H}=\hat{H}_{e}\otimes\hat{I}_{v}+\hat{H}_{ev}+\hat{I}_{e}\otimes\hat{H}_{v} \end{equation}

      • \(\hat{H}_{e}\) is the free Hamiltonian describing the energy of the isolated electron, which we will write here as

        \begin{equation} \label{orgb18e03e} \hat{H}_{e}=-\gamma\hat{\sigma}_{x}+\frac{\Delta}{2}\hat{\sigma}_{z} \end{equation}
        • Here, we are following the quantum mechanics convention for \(\hat{\sigma}_{z}\)
      • \(\hat{I}_{e}\) is the identity operator for the electron

      • \(\hat{H}_{v}\) is the vibronic (QHO) Hamiltonian

        \begin{equation} \label{orgd1b95a6} \hat{H}_{v}=-\frac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}\frac{d^{2}}{dQ^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}m\omega^{2}Q^{2}=\frac{\hat{P}_{Q}}{2m}+\frac{1}{2}m\omega^{2}\hat{Q}^{2}+\hbar\omega\left(\hat{a}_{Q}^{\dagger}\hat{a}_{Q}+\frac{1}{2}\hat{I}_{v}\right) \end{equation}

        where

        • \(\hat{Q}\) is is the vibronic position operator
        • \(\hat{P}_{Q}\) is its momentum operator
        • \(m\) is the oscillator's effective mass
        • \(\omega\) is the oscillator's frequency of oscillation
      • \(\hat{I}_{v}\) is the identity operator for the vibron
      • \(\hat{H}_{ev}\) is the interaction between the electron and vibron

        • Typically we will use the Peipho-Kraus-Stein model, where the interaction is linear in both \(\hat{\sigma}_{z}\) and \(\hat{Q}\):

          \begin{equation} \label{org8876a99} \hat{H}_{ev}=\frac{g_{ev}}{2}\hat{\sigma}_{z}\otimes\hat{Q}, \end{equation}

          where \(g_{ev}\) is the electron-vibron coupling constant, given by

          \begin{equation} g_{ev}=\sqrt{2m\omega^{2}\lambda}, \end{equation}

          and \(\lambda\) is the inner-sphere reorganization energy, a measure of how much the electron transfer affects the geometry of the molecule

    • In Equation \eqref{org91c05dd}, it is explicit that we exand both the Hamiltonian \(\hat{H}_{e}\) for the two-dimensional electronic state and the Hamiltonian \(\hat{H}_{v}\) for and \(N\)-dimensional oscillator to a larger Hilbert space of dimension \(2N\)

    • It is customary in the literature to omit the identity operators from Equation \eqref{org91c05dd} leaving them implicit rather than explicit, so that we rewrite Equation \eqref{org91c05dd} as

      \begin{equation} \label{org99aa48e} \hat{H}=\hat{H}_{e}+\hat{H}_{ev}+\hat{H}_{v}=-\gamma\hat{\sigma}_{x}+\frac{\Delta}{2}\hat{\sigma}_{z}+\frac{g_{ev}}{2}\hat{\sigma}_{z}\otimes\hat{Q}+\frac{\hat{P}_{Q}}{2m}+\frac{1}{2}m\omega^{2}\hat{Q}^{2} \end{equation}

9.2. The Statics of an Electron+vibron QCA cell

Exercise 10.1 Calculate the ground state for a driven QCA molecule, including the effects of molecular vibration. Plot the vibron probability distribution over position, and provide a sketch of the resulting electronic configuration.

Show/hide solution ...

The following code provides a solution, with results shown in Figure 48. 


import datetime as dt
import numpy as np
import scipy as sp
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import os

from qcapackage.quantum.harmonicoscillator \
    import HarmonicOscillator as QHO
from qcapackage.quantum import linearalgebra as la
from qcapackage.quantum import computing as qc
from qcapackage import constants as const
from qcapackage.quantum import visualization as viz

def dag( Q ):
    '''Calculates the Hermitian adjoint of a quantum object
       (vector or operator).

    Args:
      qobj: an numpy.ndarray representing a operator, bra,
            or ket.

    Returns:
      The Hermitian adjoint of Q.

    '''

    return np.conj( Q.transpose() )

def expectation_value( op, psi ):
    """
       Return the expectation value for an
       operator op.

       Args
         psi: a state vector

       Returns real expectation value
    """

    temp = dag( psi ) @ op @ psi

    return temp[0,0].real

# Pauli matrices
Sx, Sy, Sz = qc.Sx, qc.Sy, qc.Sz
rx, ry, rz = la.rx, la.ry, la.rz

Ie = Sx @ Sx # electronic identity operator

qe = const.qe # [C] fundamental charge
eps0 = const.eps0 # [F/m] = [C/V*m]

ket0, ket1 = qc.ket0, qc.ket1

# Projection matrices |0><0| and |1><1|
P0  = la.density_matrix( ket0 )
P1  = la.density_matrix( ket1 )

ketMinus = (1/np.sqrt(2)) * (ket0 - ket1)

# Parameters
a = 1E-9 # [m] cell characteristic length
g = 0.05 # [eV] tunneling energy
E_reorg = 0.44 # [eV] reorganization energy

Ek = 0.421 # [eV] kink energy

# Create a QHO object
N = 15 # [] dimension of oscillator
# Uses default m = 6 amu and f = 300 inv cm
qosc = QHO(N=N)

# Extract operators from the qosc object
Q0 = qosc.Q0 # [nm] characteristic length
Q = qosc.X # [nm] position operator
Qval, _ = np.linalg.eigh(Q)
Hv = qosc.H # [eV] vibronic Hamiltonian, energy basis
Iv = np.eye(N, dtype='complex')

Pdrv = 1
D = Pdrv*Ek

# [eV/nm] electron-vibron coupling constant
gev = np.sqrt( 2*qosc.m*qosc.w**2*E_reorg)

He = -g*Sx - 0.5*D*Sz # [eV] electronic

# [eV] interaction term
Hev = -0.5*gev*np.kron(Sz, Q)

# [eV] total Hamiltonian
H = np.kron(He,Iv) + np.kron(Ie,Hv) + Hev

Egnd, psi_gnd = la.ground_state( H )

rho_gnd = la.density_matrix( psi_gnd )

rho_e, rho_v = la.bipartite_traceout( rho_gnd, 2, N )

# There are two methods for calculating polarization
#    1 - project Sz into full Hilbert space
#    2 - get a reduced density operator for electron
#        use the Sz operator as before
# Here we will use method 2 so we can also see what
#  the vibron is doing

Ptgt = -la.expectation_value(Sz, rho_e)
print(f'Driver polarization: {Pdrv}; ' \
    f'target polarization: {Ptgt:5.3f}')

inset_w, inset_h = 0.65, 0.65
inset_x_l, inset_x_r = -0.125, 0.5
inset_y_b, inset_y_t = 0.125, 0.125

# Lower right inset - borrow previously-created inset
Pd, Pt = 1, -1
ins_name = os.path.join('img', f'inset_driven_dqd_{Pd}_{Pt}.png')

plt.close('all')
fig, ax = plt.subplots(2, 1)
ax[0].bar( Qval/Q0, np.diag( qosc.change_basis(rho_v,
                                              fr='energy',
                                              to='position') ) )
ax[0].set_ylabel('Probability', fontsize=18)
ax[0].grid(True)

# Add a QCA inset images
viz.add_inset_image( ax[0],
                    [inset_x_l, inset_y_b, inset_w, inset_h],
                    ins_name )

# Case 2
Pdrv = -1
D = Pdrv*Ek

He = -g*Sx - 0.5*D*Sz # [eV] electronic

# [eV] total Hamiltonian
H = np.kron(He,Iv) + np.kron(Ie,Hv) + Hev

Egnd, psi_gnd = la.ground_state( H )

rho_gnd = la.density_matrix( psi_gnd )

rho_e, rho_v = la.bipartite_traceout( rho_gnd, 2, N )

Ptgt = -la.expectation_value(Sz, rho_e)
print(f'Driver polarization: {Pdrv}; ' \
    + f'target polarization: {Ptgt:5.3f}')

# Lower right inset - borrow previously-created inset
Pd, Pt = -1, 1
ins_name = os.path.join('img',
                        f'inset_driven_dqd_{Pd}_{Pt}.png')

ax[1].bar( Qval/Q0, np.diag( qosc.change_basis(rho_v,
                                              fr='energy',
                                              to='position') ) )
ax[1].set_xlabel(r'$Q/Q_0$', fontsize=18)
ax[1].grid(True)

# Add the two QCA inset images
viz.add_inset_image( ax[1],
                    [inset_x_r, inset_y_b, inset_w, inset_h],
                    ins_name )

ax[1].set_xlabel(r'$Q/Q_0$')
ax[1].set_ylabel('Probability', fontsize=18)
plt.tight_layout()

fname = os.path.join('img', f'driven_electron_vibron_distrib.png')

plt.savefig( fname )
plt.close('all')

print(f'\n[[./{fname}]]')

print(f'\nCode executed at {dt.datetime.now()}')

Driver polarization: 1; target polarization: -0.993
