Code executed at 2026-09-08 05:49:22.737676

Figure 48: Dynamics calculated using the Lindblad equation reflect damping and dissipation. (a) The QHO modeled using the quantum Liouville equation behaves as a closed-system, in which oscillations persist forever. In the case of Lindblad evolution, however, damping is evident as oscillations decrease in amplitude with time. (b) In the energy basis, Liouvillian evolution conserves energy, but an exponential decrease in energy is evident in the open system modeled using the Lindblad equation.

  • Observations:
    • The bias on the electron results in a well-defined QCA device state
    • The state of the electron couples to state of the vibron, shifting it to one side of \(Q=0\)
    • Reversing the driver state reverses the bias on the target molecule and causes the opposite response in the molecular bonds

Exercise 10.2 Calculate the cell-cell response for a driven QCA molecule, including the effects of molecular vibration. Compare this result to that of a QCA cell without any molecular vibrations.

Show/hide solution ...

The following code provides a solution, shown in Figure 49. 


import datetime as dt
import numpy as np
import scipy as sp
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import os

from qcapackage.quantum.harmonicoscillator \
  import HarmonicOscillator as QHO
from qcapackage.quantum import linearalgebra as la
from qcapackage.quantum import computing as qc
from qcapackage import constants as const
from qcapackage.quantum import visualization as viz

def dag( Q ):
  '''Calculates the Hermitian adjoint of a quantum object
     (vector or operator).

  Args:
    qobj: an numpy.ndarray representing a operator, bra,
          or ket.

  Returns:
    The Hermitian adjoint of Q.

  '''

  return np.conj( Q.transpose() )

def expectation_value( op, psi ):
  """
     Return the expectation value for an
     operator op.

     Args
       psi: a state vector

     Returns real expectation value
  """

  temp = dag( psi ) @ op @ psi

  return temp[0,0].real

# Pauli matrices
Sx, Sy, Sz = qc.Sx, qc.Sy, qc.Sz
rx, ry, rz = la.rx, la.ry, la.rz

Ie = Sx @ Sx # electronic identity operator

qe = const.qe # [C] fundamental charge
eps0 = const.eps0 # [F/m] = [C/V*m]

ket0, ket1 = qc.ket0, qc.ket1

# Projection matrices |0><0| and |1><1|
P0  = la.density_matrix( ket0 )
P1  = la.density_matrix( ket1 )

ketMinus = (1/np.sqrt(2)) * (ket0 - ket1)

# Parameters
a = 1E-9 # [m] cell characteristic length
g = 0.05 # [eV] tunneling energy
E_reorg = 0.44 # [eV] reorganization energy
nPdrv = 35

Ek = 0.421 # [eV] kink energy
Pdrv = np.linspace(-1, 1, nPdrv)

# Create a QHO object
N = 15 # [] dimension of oscillator
# Uses default m = 6 amu and f = 300 inv cm
qosc = QHO(N=N)

# Extract operators from the qosc object
Q0 = qosc.Q0 # [nm] characteristic length
Q = qosc.X # [nm] position operator
Qval, _ = np.linalg.eigh(Q)
Hv = qosc.H # [eV] vibronic Hamiltonian, energy basis
Iv = np.eye(N, dtype='complex')

# [eV/nm] electron-vibron coupling constant
gev = np.sqrt( 2*qosc.m*qosc.w**2*E_reorg)

# [eV] interaction term
Hev = -0.5*gev*np.kron(Sz, Q)

Ptgt_vib = np.zeros((nPdrv,)) # electron coupled to vibron
Ptgt = np.zeros((nPdrv,)) # free QCA system - no vibrons

for iPdrv, Pdval in enumerate(Pdrv):
  D = Pdval*Ek
  # Bias affects electron Hamiltonian
  He = -g*Sx - 0.5*D*Sz # [eV] electronic

  # [eV] total Hamiltonian
  H = np.kron(He,Iv) + np.kron(Ie,Hv) + Hev

  Egnd, psi_gnd = la.ground_state( H )

  rho_gnd = la.density_matrix( psi_gnd )

  rho_e, rho_v = la.bipartite_traceout( rho_gnd, 2, N )

  Ptgt_vib[iPdrv] = -la.expectation_value(Sz, rho_e)

  Egnd_no_vib, psi_gnd_novib = la.ground_state(He)

  Ptgt[iPdrv] = -la.expectation_value(Sz, psi_gnd_novib)

inset_w, inset_h = 0.65, 0.65
inset_x_l, inset_x_r = -0.125, 0.5
inset_y_b, inset_y_t = 0.125, 0.125

# Lower right inset - borrow previously-created inset
Pd, Pt = 1, -1
ins_name = os.path.join('img', f'inset_driven_dqd_{Pd}_{Pt}.png')

plt.close('all')
fig, ax = plt.subplots()

ax.plot(Pdrv, Ptgt_vib, label='with vibron', marker='*')
ax.plot(Pdrv, Ptgt, label='without vibron', marker='o')
ax.set_ylabel(r'$P_{tgt}$', fontsize=18)
ax.set_xlabel(r'$P_{drv}$', fontsize=18)
ax.grid(True)
ax.legend()

# Add a QCA inset images
'''
viz.add_inset_image( ax[0],
                  [inset_x_l, inset_y_b, inset_w, inset_h],
                  ins_name )
'''

plt.tight_layout()

fname = os.path.join('img', f'driven_electron_vibron_cell-cell-pol.png')

plt.savefig( fname )
plt.close('all')

print(f'\n[[./{fname}]]')

print(f'\nCode executed at {dt.datetime.now()}')


[[./img/driven_electron_vibron_cell-cell-pol.png]]

Code executed at 2026-09-08 05:49:25.539371

Figure 49: Electron-vibron coupling enhances the cell-cell response. This is done through a phenomena called the self-trapping of charge. The reorganization of atoms due to the electron transfer enhances the trapping of charge on a dot, leading to better localization and a stronger polarization response.

9.3. Dynamics: A Driven Cell with an Abrupt Driver Switch

Exercise 10.1 Let us prepare a target cell by setting a nearby a driver cell to have the state \(P_{\text{drv}}\left(t<0\right)=1\), and let us assume that at \(t=0\), the target cell is in steady state. Find the electronic polarization and probability distribution over space as functions of time for \(t>0\) if at \(t=0,\) the driver state is abruptly switched to \(P_{\text{drv}}\left(t>0\right)=-1\). The initial preparation and the abrupt driver switch at \(t=0\), and the final configuration are shown in Figures 50-52. In more detail:

  1. The driver is set to state \(P_{drv}=-1\) in order to prepare a vibronic QCA cell to be in state \(\ket{0}\) with \(P=1\), as shown in Figure 50. The vibronic QCA cell is relaxed close to its ground state.
  2. At \(t=0,\) the driver is abruptly switched to \(P_{drv}=1\). This injects a kink of energy into the system, exciting the target cell by \(E_{k}\). See Figure 51 What happens next (\(t>0^{+}\))?

  3. After a long time, the electron has transferred to the other dot, and the QCA has achieved a lower-energy configuration by releasing energy to the environment as heat. The final configuration is shown in Figure 52.

    Figure 50: \(t<0\)

    Figure 51: \(t=0^{+}\)

Figure 52: \(t\rightarrow\infty\)

Show/hide solution ...

The following code provides a solution, shown in Figure 53. 


import datetime as dt
import numpy as np
import scipy as sp
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import os

from qcapackage.qca.vibronicdqd import VibronicDQD as VDQD
from qcapackage.qca.twodotcell import TwoDotCell as DQD
import qcapackage.quantum.linearalgebra as la

from qcapackage.quantum.timeevolution import lindblad
from odeintw import odeintw

# ==================================================
# Step 1 - Prepare the ground state
# ==================================================
Qdrv = DQD(position=np.array([[-1],[0],[0]]))
Qdrv.polarization = 1

Qtgt = VDQD()
# Accumulate potential on target due to driver
Qtgt.accumulate_potential_from(Qdrv)

# The initial state for our Lindbladian evolution
# will be the ground state for Pdrv = -1
psi0 = Qtgt.ground_state()
rho0 = la.density_matrix(psi0)

# ==================================================
# Step 2 - Calculate dynamics for t > 0
# ==================================================

# switch the driver
Qdrv.polarization = -1

# Recalculate driver potential
Qtgt.zero_neighbor_potential()
Qtgt.accumulate_potential_from(Qdrv)

# Lindbladian evolution (not using Qutip)
Qtgt.make_lindblad_operators() # default is thermalization

# We'll use H to extract information
H = Qtgt.get_h()

T = Qtgt.qho.T # [s] Oscillator period

nt = 250
t = np.linspace(0, 50*T, nt)

# Calculate thermal density matrix
#   This might be the steady-state solution
rho_th = la.thermal_density_matrix( 300, H )
_, rhov_th = la.bipartite_traceout( rho_th,
              Qtgt.dimension,
              Qtgt.qho.N)
Eexpth = la.expectation_value( H, rho_th)
Qexpth = la.expectation_value( Qtgt.qho.X, rhov_th )

Egs, psigs = la.ground_state( H )
rhogs = la.density_matrix( psigs )

_, rhovgs = la.bipartite_traceout( rhogs,
              Qtgt.dimension,
              Qtgt.qho.N)
Qexpgs = la.expectation_value( Qtgt.qho.X, rhovgs )

# sol - solution to the Lindblad equation
sol = odeintw(lindblad, rho0, t,
     args=(H, Qtgt.LindbladOps))

# Storage arrays for time dynamics
PDX = np.zeros( [Qtgt.qho.N, nt] )
Xexp = np.zeros( (nt, ) )
Pexp = np.zeros( (nt, ) )
Eexp = np.zeros( (nt, ) )

# ==================================================
# Step 3 - Post-processing
# ==================================================
# extract data from sol array
for it, rhot in enumerate(sol):

    rhote, rhotv = la.bipartite_traceout( rhot,
                                         Qtgt.dimension,
                                         Qtgt.qho.N )

    Eexp[it] = la.expectation_value( H, rhot )
    Xexp[it] = la.expectation_value( Qtgt.qho.X, rhotv )
    Pexp[it] = la.expectation_value( Qtgt.Zop.full(), rhote )

    rhotvX = Qtgt.qho.change_basis( rhotv,
                               fr='energy',
                               to='position' )

    PDX[:,it] = np.diag(rhotvX).real

# ==================================================
# Step 4 - Visualization
# ==================================================
# 2D arrays for plotting
tt, XX = np.meshgrid( t/T,
                      Qtgt.qho.Xval/Qtgt.qho.Q0,
                      indexing='xy' )

plt.close('all')

fig, ax = plt.subplots(2, 1)

ax[0].plot(t/T, Pexp)
ax[0].set_xlim([t[0]/T, t[-1]/T])
ax[0].grid(True)
ax[0].set_ylabel('$P$', fontsize=16)

ax[1].contourf( tt, XX, PDX, levels=75)
ax[1].plot( t/T, Xexp/Qtgt.qho.Q0, color='white' )
ax[1].set_xlabel(r'$t/T$', fontsize=16)
ax[1].set_ylabel(r'$\left< Q \right>/Q_o $', fontsize=16)

plt.tight_layout()
fname = os.path.join('img', 'VibronicDQD_abrupt_switch.png')

plt.savefig(fname)

print(f'\n[[./{fname}]]')

plt.close("all")

fig, ax = plt.subplots()
ax.plot(t/T, Eexp)
ax.set_xlim([t[0]/T, t[-1]/T])
ax.grid(True)
ax.set_ylabel('$E$ (eV)', fontsize=16)
ax.set_xlabel(r'$t/T$', fontsize=16)

plt.tight_layout()
fname = os.path.join('img', 'VibronicDQD_abrupt_switch_energy.png')

plt.savefig(fname)

print(f'\n[[./{fname}]]')

plt.close("all")

print(f'\nCode executed at {dt.datetime.now()}')

Figure 53: Electron-vibron coupling enhances the cell-cell response. This is done through a phenomena called the self-trapping of charge. The reorganization of atoms due to the electron transfer enhances the trapping of charge on a dot, leading to better localization and a stronger polarization response.

10. Programming in Python

10.1. Python Basics

  • Python is a wildly popular, versatile cross-platform computer programming language supported in Windows, Mac OS, and UNIX/Linux
  • Numerous open-source packages are available for pre-built functionality, but you may have to install them. These include:
    • numpy for numerical tools, such as vectors, matrices, and trigonometric functions
    • matplotlib for MATLAB-like plotting
    • qutip for modeling quantum systems
    • qiskit for programming quantum computers

10.2. Obtaining Python

  • I'm recommending obtaining Anaconda, a distribution made with natural scientists, engineers, and data scientists (and others) in mind
    • At this time, Python 2 and 3 are in use and available in Anaconda
      • I prefer Python 3, because it is the direction of the future
      • Python 2 is becoming depreciated, and Python 3 is becoming increasingly dominant

10.3. Installation

  • Obtain Anaconda from their downloads page
  • You may see my video: Install Python on Mac or PC (for FREE)

10.3.1. Windows Installation

  • Windows users may download Anaconda and follow the (fairly) intuitive installation process

10.3.2. Mac Installation

  • For Mac users, I recommend the command-line installation (not the graphical installation), since I've had the best luck with this when trying install packages

  • Before getting into this, I suggest being familiar with the Terminal. Others have video intros to the terminal, but so do I. Feel free to find another if this isn't helpful:

  • Once you can Terminal, you can follow the Anaconda Mac Command-line Interface (CLI) installation instructions.

10.3.3. UNIX/Linux

  • I have little experience with Linux, so I won't attempt to guide you here.

10.4. Test Your Python

  • Now, you have to test your Python
    • To do this, you have to run your Python
    • A nice way to run Python is in Jupyter Lab
  • To run Python, you can do the following
    • On a Mac
      • Open a Terminal window
      • type jupyter lab followed by RETURN to launch Jupyter Lab
    • On a PC
      • Start the Anaconda Navigator
      • Launch Jupyter Lab
  • Once you have opened Jupyter Lab, a new web browser will arise with a Jupyter Lab graphical interface
    • You can begin coding once the Jupyter Lab window is open!

  • If you need help starting Jupyter Lab, please see my video: Launch Jupyter Lab and Start Coding! 

    print('hello world')

    hello world
  • My "hello world" is the single line in the listing above
  • Observations:
    • There is a print() command with a single argument
      • That argument is a string, which is a sequence of characters
      • You can define a string by putting the character sequence between a pair single quotes ('some text') or double quotes (i.e, "some text" - chose one or the other, but not both to define your string)
    • print() does one thing: it makes the input string visible in the Python output

  • Another check you can perform is to make Python print its version, as shown here 

    import sys # make the sys package available for use
print(sys.version) # print the version information

    3.11.7 (main, Dec 15 2023, 12:09:56) [Clang 14.0.6 ]
  • This code uses the built-in sys module to query the Python version, and print() displays the result.
    • We see that I'm using Python 3, version 3.11.7.
    • The # character is the beginning of an inline comment
      • The # character an any subsequent characters are not evaluated
      • Comments are strictly for the human reader to aid in code understandability (very important)
      • Comments clarify code to others and clarify a thought process to oneself

10.5. Making a Virtual Environment

  • See also my video, Customizing Python in Anaconda - Environments and Package Installation
  • Virtual environments may be thought of as a particular installation of Python on your computer with a particular set of packages installed
    • Your computer can host several virtual environments, but we typically use only one environment at a time
  • It is a good practice to use a separate virtual environment for each Python project
    • With multiple projects, the required packages may conflict
    • For example
      • Project 1 might require packages A and B
      • Project 2 might require packages A and C
      • Sometimes, we may have conflicts
        • Package C might conflict with package B
        • Packages B and C might require different versions of package A
    • Virtual environments avoid conflicts by allowing you to use different package sets for individual projects
      • This also makes your work more reproducible with a requirements.txt file
        • This file specifies the specific packages you use in your environment and their versions
        • You can then share your requirements.txt file with others, and along with your Python code developed within that environment, and everything should work harmoniously on your collaborator's computer
      • Anaconda virtual environments even give you the ability to specify different versions of Python
  • Anaconda comes with a package manager called conda
    • conda can create/manage environments and packages
  • See also my video, Customizing Python in Anaconda - Environments and Package Installation

10.5.1. Creating Environments

  • To create an environment:
    • Open a terminal application
      • On Windows, start the Anaconda Navigator and launch the Powershell application
      • On a Mac, open a Terminal (or iTerm2) window
    • In the terminal, type conda create --name <EnvName> python=3.10 and press RETURN (ENTER) to execue the command
      • Here, you replace <EnvName> the the specific name you choose for your environment
        • Short, simple names will be most effective
      • This creates your environment and specifies that we want to use Python 3.10
        • I've found that newer versions of Python don't work well with Qiskit
  • You can verify that your environment is created by typing conda info --envs
    • If the environment appears in the resulting list, you have successfully created it
    • Otherwise, you may need to try again

10.5.2. Activating Your Environment

  • After creating your environment–especially if this is the first environment you've created–it is likely that your base environment is active
    • You can tell this by looking at the start of your prompt string
      • If it says (base), then, then your base environment is active
  • To activate you environment, type at the Powershell or Terminal prompt:
    conda activate <EnvName>
    • This activates your environment so that you can:
      • install packages or
      • use the packages you previously installed in that environment
  • You can deactivate your environment by typing conda deactivate at the Powershell/Terminal prompt

10.5.3. Installing Packages

  • You must sometimes install packages for your new environment to add capabilities
  • Here, we will focus on the following:
    • jupyterlab - allows for an interactive session in your new environment (it's installed by default in the base environment)
    • qiskit - allows you to develop quantum computing circuits
    • qiskit-aer - allows you to simulate quantum circuits
    • matplotlib - helps you visualize quantum circuits
    • pylatexenc - helps you visualize quantum circuits in a nicer way than the default
  • For a walk-through on the package installation, see my video A Python Environment for Quantum Computing
  • To install packages in an environment:
    • Open the Powershell or Terminal as needed
    • Activate the desired environment
    • For each package to be installed:
      • First, try typing conda install <packagename>
        • When prompted, type y + RETURN
  • Troubleshooting
    • If conda install <packagename> doesn't work (you see a failed to resolve the environment, try searching for a specific installation command, such as conda install conda-forge::pylatexenc=in the case of =pylatexenc
      • This points example points to an installation on the conda-forge package collection
    • If conda install ... fails despite specifying a package collection, try installing with pip install ... instead of conda install .... As a note, you need not specify a repository like conda-forge when you use pip install ...

10.5.4. Listing Installed Packages

  • To install packages installed in the active environment, type conda list in the Powershell/Terminal prompt

10.6. Python Programming Basics

10.6.1. Level 0: Python as a Calculator

  • The simplest way to use Python is as a calculator
  • Some basic arithmetic operations include addition (+), subtraction (-), multiplication (*), division (/), modulus (remainder, %), integer division (//), and exponentiation (**)

  • These are illustrated in the following listing 

    print(3+5)
print(3-5)
print(3*5)
print(5/3)
print(5%3)
print(5//3)
print(3**5)

    8
-2
15
1.6666666666666667
2
1
243
  • Notice that here, the input to print() is not a string, but a mathematical expression.
    • Python is doing the following in each print() command:
      • Evaluating each mathematical expression
      • Automatically converting the result to a string, which is the input for print().

  • For improved readability of the results, we can combine the arithmetic expression with a string using the .format() method, as illustrated here: 

    print('3+5 = {0}'.format(3+5))
print('3-5 = {0}; 5/3 = {1}'.format(3-5, 5/3))
print('3-5 = {0}; 5/3 = {1:06.3f}'.format(3-5, 5/3)) # nicer formatting

    3+5 = 8
3-5 = -2; 5/3 = 1.6666666666666667
3-5 = -2; 5/3 = 01.667
  • Observations
    • There is a string as the input to the print() function
    • Each string has braces {n} with a non-negative integer index (\(n\))
      • Each indexed pair of braces is a placeholder for an embedded value
      • The .format() method specifies a list of values for the various placeholders
      • The \(k\)-th value from the list goes in placeholder indexed with \(k-1\).
    • Line 3 of the above listing is the same as line 2, but with a small change: for placeholder 1, we add :06.3f
      • The ":" indicates the presence of a formatting string, 06.3f
      • 0 allows for leading zeros (not used in this case). This could be omitted if we don't want leading zeros
      • 6 reserves a total 6 characters for the placeholder, including the decimal
      • .3 reserves 3 characters for the decimal portion of the number
      • f specifies that we are expecting a floating-point number format (we could also specify d for an integer format or s if we want to put a string in the placeholder)
      • The formatting string is optional. Without it, Python uses default behaviors to display the value assigned to the placeholder.

10.6.2. Level 1: Variables in Python

  • Your powers increase and life becomes better when you can use variables
    • Variables are defined on the fly (no previous declarations are needed) using the assignment operator (=)
    • From left to right, provide a valid variable name , an assignment operator, and a value or expression
      • Any value provided will be stored in memory, accessible using the variable name you provided
      • Any expression will be evaluated and converted to a value, and that value will be stored in memory, accessible using the same variable name.

  • Some examples are provided in this listing: 

    a = 3 # assign the value 3 to variable a
b = 5 # assign 5 to variable b
print('a+b = {0}'.format(a+b))

c = a - b # evaluate a-b, store in c
d = a/b # evaluate a/b, store in d

# Make an f-string
myFirstFStr = f'c = {c}; d = {d:5.3f}'
print(myFirstFStr) # print the f-string

myStr = 'cat' # store a string
mySecondFStr = f'random word: {myStr}'
print(mySecondFStr)

# Example: complex numbers
X = 3 + 2j # complex number
absX = abs(X)

# print the complex X using an f-string
#   form the f-string inside of print()
print(f'X = {X}; |X| = {absX:06.3f}')

    a+b = 8
c = -2; d = 0.600
random word: cat
X = (3+2j); |X| = 03.606

  • Here, we also use f-strings (formatted string literals)

    • To make an f-string, we prepend an f onto a string definition, as in line 9
    • Use the syntax {x} within the f-string to embed the string representation of some variable, x,
    • The f-string may be printed, as in line 10
    • An f-string may be formed inside a print() command, as in line 22
    • For more information on f-strings, see the Python documentation

  • One neat trick in Python is that we can define several variables using only one assignment statement: 

    x, y, z = 1+2, 2*3, 4/5
print(f'x = {x}; y = {y}; z = {z:5.3f}')

    x = 3; y = 6; z = 0.800
  • Here, we assign three variables using one assignment operator (=)
    • We follow the assignment with a print() statement so that we can verify that our multivariable assignment worked
  • We recognize that variables may have different types
    • Each variable type–or more generally, class (more on this later)–has a different syntax and behaviors governing what we can do with variables of that type
  • We've encountered some different variable types by now:
    • strings - store lists of characters, as in text
    • integers - (self explanatory)
    • floating point numbers (self explanatory)
    • complex numbers - (self explanatory)
  • We can interrogate the type of a variable, say, x, using the command type(x)

  • Examples of different variables and their type ("class") are shown here: 

    a, b, c, d = 1, 3.14, 2+3j, False

print('type of a: {0}'.format(type(a)))
print('type of b: {0}'.format(type(b)))
print('type of c: {0}'.format(type(c)))
print('type of d: {0}'.format(type(d)))

    type of a: <class 'int'>
type of b: <class 'float'>
type of c: <class 'complex'>
type of d: <class 'bool'>
  • In the above listing, we introduce another data type: bool, short for Boolean
    • Boolean variables have one of two values: True, or False
    • These may be used to control wheter sections of code get executed or skipped, using logical control statements (a.k.a. logical controls)
  • Boolean variables are typically the result of a comparison
    • Comparisons may be performed between pairs of variables or values
      • Here, we must use comparison operators such as < (less than), > (greater than), == (equal to), >= (grater than or equal to), <= (grater than or equal to), != (not equal to), etc.
    • Comparison operators are used to form logical expressions, which are evaluated as either True or False. Some examples:
      • The logical expression 3<5 will evaluate to True, but 3>5 will evaluate to False
      • a==b may evaluate either to True or False, depending on the values stored in a and b.
        • If the two values are equal, then this statement evaluates to True
        • otherwise, it evaluates to False
      • The value resulting from a logical expression may be:
        • stored in a variable, which will be of the type bool
        • used in a logical control structure, which determines whether or not a block of code gets executed

  • Example code involving =bool=s and comparison is given here: 

    a = 3 < 5
b = 5 < 3
c, d, f = 3 > 5, 3 < 5, 3==5

print('type of a: {0}; a = {1}'.format(type(a), a))
print('type of b: {0}; b = {1}'.format(type(b), b))
print(f'c = {c}; d = {d}; f = {f}')

    type of a: <class 'bool'>; a = True
type of b: <class 'bool'>; b = False
c = False; d = True; f = False

10.6.3. Level 2: if statements

  • if statements allow you to execute blocks of code depending on the value of a logical expression
  • The basic if statement:
    • begins with if
    • identifies a variable or condition for evaluation, and
    • ends with a colon (:)

  • An example is given here: 

    x = True # define a boolean (bool) variable

if x:
   # This doesn't print if x is set to False
   print('The condition x evaluates as True.')
   # We could include other commands in the IF block
   # We only use one command here
   # Additional commands must have the same indentation
   #   as the print() command

print('This is independent of the IF statement.')

    The condition x evaluates as True.
This is independent of the IF statement.

  • We now repeat the code of of the previous listing: 

    x = False # define a boolean (bool) variable

if x:
   # This doesn't print if x is set to False
   print('The condition x evaluates as True.')
   # We could include other commands in the IF block
   # We only use one command here
   # Additional commands must have the same indentation
   #   as the print() command

print('This is independent of the IF statement.')

    This is independent of the IF statement.
  • Here, the if statement condition is evaluated as False (we set it to False in line 1), so the print() command conditioned upon the it is never executed, and we don't see its output
  • Commands subordinate to the if statement follow it and are defined by their indentation.
    • The first line of code with the same indentation as the if statement is outside of the if statement.
    • Unlike other languages, the Python if statement is not defined using an end statement or a closing delimiter, but rather by returning to the level of indentation of the if statement itself.
  1. if-else Statements
    • We can add to an if SomeExpression: block an else: block that provides code to be executed if the controlling logical expression SomeExpression: is False

    • An example of this is given here: 

      y = False

if y:
    print('The condition y evaluates as True.')
else:
    print('The condition y evaluates as False.')

# The if statement is now over
print('This is independent of the IF-ELSE statement.')

      The condition y evaluates as False.
This is independent of the IF-ELSE statement.
    1. if-elif-else Statements
      • We will use an if-elif-else statement to illustrate the use of the None value
      • Python's elif is equivalent to else if or similar constructs in other languages.

      • Here is an example: 

        x = None

if x is None:
    y = 100
elif x < 50:
    y = 200
else:
    y = 300

# We're outside of the if-elif-else statement now
print(f'y = {y}')

        y = 100
  2. None in Logical Expressions

    • Here is another example using is not None as the controlling logical expression: 

      y = True

if y is not None:
    print('y has a value.')

      y has a value.

10.6.4. Level 3: Iterables

  • Iterables are variables that contain other variables.
    • These are useful for storing multiple related values
    • Iterables are so called because they are collections of items over which we may iterate (as, in a for loop, for example)
  1. Strings
    • Strings are iterables.
      • A string, 'cat', for example, is a collection of characters.
      • For a string of \(n\) characters, each character can be addressed using an integer index, \(\left\{ 0,1,2,\ldots,n-1\right\}\).
      • For a string stored in the variable X, we reference each element using brackets ([ and ]) and an integer index, k.

      • Here is an example with output 

        X = 'dog'

print('The first letter of X is "{0}".'.format(X[0]))
print('The second letter of X is "{0}".'.format(X[1]))

        The first letter of X is "d".
The second letter of X is "o".
    • Now, consider a longer string
      • We may not necessarily know a priori how long it is
      • We can easily address its end element using the index -1, or the second-to-last character using the index -2
      • Additionally, we can reference multiple contiguous elements using a range, a:b, with integers a and b.
        • This returns elements X[a] through X[b-1] for a string X.

      • Examples of additional string indexing are shown here 

        X = 'hello world'

# We can find the length of the string using len()
print('X contains {0} characters.'.format( len(X) ))

# reference from the begining of the string
print('\nThe first letter of X is "{0}".'.format(X[0]))
print('The second letter of X is "{0}".'.format(X[1]))

# reference from the end of the string
print('\nThe last letter of X is "{0}".'.format(X[-1]))
print('The second-to-last letter of X is "{0}".'.format(X[-2]))

# slice part of the string
a, b = 2, 9
S = X[a:b]
print(f'\nHere, we reference elements {a}:{b}: "{S}"')

c, d = -7, -2
R = X[c:d]
print(f'\nHere, we reference elements {c}:{d}: "{R}"')

        X contains 11 characters.

The first letter of X is "h".
The second letter of X is "e".

The last letter of X is "d".
The second-to-last letter of X is "l".

Here, we reference elements 2:9: "llo wor"

Here, we reference elements -7:-2: "o wor"
  2. Lists
    • Lists are container variables used to contain other variables.
    • Lists are flexible in that:
      • A single list may contain variables of different types
      • A list may contain other lists
    • A list of several elements may be defined using brackets ([ and ]) comma-separated elements.
      • The elements are indexed with an integer 0, 1, 2, …
      • Individual elements may be accessed using brackets and an indexing integer or expression.
      • Elements may also be referenced using negative indices:
        • -1 specifies the last element;
        • -2 specifies the second-to-last element, etc.

    • Here is an example: 

      x = 9
myList = [x, 's', -3, 'cat', [1,2,3]] # form a list

# print the first element
print('The first element is: {0}'.format( myList[0] ) )

# print the second element
print('The second element is: {0}'.format( myList[1] ) )

# print the last element
print('The last element is: {0}'.format( myList[-1] ) )

# print the second-to-last element
print('The second-to-the-last element is: {0}'.format( myList[-2] ) )

      The first element is: 9
The second element is: s
The last element is: [1, 2, 3]
The second-to-the-last element is: cat
  3. Tuples
    • Tuples are like lists.
      • They are indexed in the same manner, but tuples are immutable.
    • Tuples are defined using parentheses ( and ) instead of brackets [ and ].
    • Tuples are useful when you have a collection of variables and you do not want them altered in any way.

    • Here is an example: 

      x = 9
myTup = (x, 's', -3, 'cat', [1,2,3]) # form a tuple

# print the first element
print('The first element is: {0}'.format( myTup[0] ) )

# print the second element
print('The second element is: {0}'.format( myTup[1] ) )

# print the last element
print('The last element is: {0}'.format( myTup[-1] ) )

# print the second-to-last element
print('The second-to-the-last element is: {0}'.format( myTup[-2] ) )

      The first element is: 9
The second element is: s
The last element is: [1, 2, 3]
The second-to-the-last element is: cat
  4. Dicts
    • Dict is short for “dictionary”.
      • This is like a list, but elements are not indexed by number, but rather using a string.
      • The referncing/indexing string is called a key, and the associated element is called a value.
      • Thus, a dict contains key-value pairs (KPVs).
    • We define a dict using braces { and }
      • KVPs are associated using a colon (:), and
      • KVPs are separated by a comma
      • dicts may be broken up over several lines of code, as can lists and tuples
      • Use spaces for readability

    • An example of a simple dict definition is given in this listing 

      someDict = {"a": 3,
            "b": 'dog',
            "f": 5.4 }

"""
   I used single quotes and double quotes in
   various places to demonstrate their interchangeability.
"""

print("someDict['a'] = {0}".format(someDict['a']) )
print("someDict['f'] = {0}".format(someDict["f"]) )
print('someDict["b"] = {0}'.format(someDict['b']) )

# Now I add a value to the dict
someDict['d'] = ('New value', 19)

# Finally, I show what it looks like to print a dict
#    and we show the modified dict.
print(someDict)

      someDict['a'] = 3
someDict['f'] = 5.4
someDict["b"] = dog
{'a': 3, 'b': 'dog', 'f': 5.4, 'd': ('New value', 19)}
    • Dicts are mutable: KVPs may be added, removed, or modified.

10.6.5. Level 4: Loops and Iteration

  • Loops help us perform repeated operations over an iterable.
    • Iterables include lists, tuples, dicts, and ranges.
  1. Defining a for loop
    • Some key features of a for loop:
      • The first line of a for loop
        • begins with the keyword for,
        • defines an iterator variable, say, x
        • provides an iterable, say, the list X
        • and ends with a colon (:)
      • Statements to be included in the for loop follow the first line and are indented by a few spaces more than is the first line (for x in X:)
        • I typically use an indentation of four spaces.
      • The first statement with equal or lesser indentation than the for statement is recognized by Python to be outside of the for loop.

    • As a first example, I define a list and then use two statements to print the elements of the list: 

      X = [1, 'a', (3,2,1), 'fish'] # define a list

# use a FOR loop to print the elements of the list
for ix in X:
    print('  Element:')  # in the loop
    print(f'    {ix}')   # in the loop

print('The loop has terminated.') # first statement after loop
print('Program complete.')

        Element:
    1
  Element:
    a
  Element:
    (3, 2, 1)
  Element:
    fish
The loop has terminated.
Program complete.
    1. A for loop with an index
      • Sometimes we don't just want to loop over the elements, but we also want an integer index for each element.

      • To do this, we can use enumerate(), as shown here: 

        X = [5, 3, 7, 9, 8]

Z = [] # empty list
for idx, x in enumerate(X):
    print(f'X[{idx}] = {x}')

        X[0] = 5
X[1] = 3
X[2] = 7
X[3] = 9
X[4] = 8
      • Notice that Python is a zero-indexed language, where the index starts at zero.
        • This is like C and C++, and many other languages.
      • Some languages, such as Matlab or Maple, are one-indexed.
    2. A for loop with an index - using a Common Index
      • Sometimes we want to use multiple data sets.
      • For example, we may want to add the elements of a list X to the corresponding elements of another list, Y.
        • We could use an enumerate object, as before; or,
        • we may use a range object to obtain an index to address corresponding elements of the various lists.

      • I'll demonstrate the use of a range here: 

        X = [5, 3, 7, 9, 8]
Y = [1, 4, 2, 11, 6]

Z = [] # empty list
for idx in range(0, len(X)):
    newZelement = X[idx] + Y[idx]
    Z.append( newZelement ) # add newZelement to Z

    print('X[{0}] + Y[{0}] = {1} + {2} = {3}'.format(
                idx, X[idx], Y[idx], Z[idx]) )

print(Z)

        X[0] + Y[0] = 5 + 1 = 6
X[1] + Y[1] = 3 + 4 = 7
X[2] + Y[2] = 7 + 2 = 9
X[3] + Y[3] = 9 + 11 = 20
X[4] + Y[4] = 8 + 6 = 14
[6, 7, 9, 20, 14]
      • We make a range that starts at 0 and ends at a number just high enough to capture all the elements of X.
        • We also assume that X and Y have the same number of elements.
      • The loop contains three instructions:
        • Add the indexed elements of X and Y, storing this result in newZelement
        • Append newZelement to Z.
        • Print data so we know it worked properly.

      • We could combine the first two instructions into one : 

        X = [5, 3, 7, 9, 8]
Y = [1, 4, 2, 11, 6]

Z = [] # empty list
for idx in range(0, len(X)):
    newZelement = X[idx] + Y[idx]
    Z.append( newZelement ) # add newZelement to Z

    print('X[{0}] + Y[{0}] = {1} + {2} = {3}'.format(
                idx, X[idx], Y[idx], Z[idx]) )

print(Z)

        X[0] + Y[0] = 5 + 1 = 6
X[1] + Y[1] = 3 + 4 = 7
X[2] + Y[2] = 7 + 2 = 9
X[3] + Y[3] = 9 + 11 = 20
X[4] + Y[4] = 8 + 6 = 14
[6, 7, 9, 20, 14]
    3. A FOR loop without an index
      • We may work with two data lists without making a common index.

      • In this technique, we iterate over two lists simultaneously using zip: 

        X = [5, 3, 7, 9, 8]
Y = [1, 4, 2, 11, 6]

Z = [] # empty list
for x,y in zip(X, Y):
    Z.append( x + y ) # add to Z

    print('x + y = {0} + {1} = {2}'.format(
                x, y, Z[-1]) )

print(Z)

        x + y = 5 + 1 = 6
x + y = 3 + 4 = 7
x + y = 7 + 2 = 9
x + y = 9 + 11 = 20
x + y = 8 + 6 = 14
[6, 7, 9, 20, 14]
    4. Calculating Data from Members of a List - A Traditional Method

      The first method we showcase here is very intuitive: it is the way we might do this in another language. We start with a list, X, and we calculate a new list Y, whose elements correspond to the square of the elements in X: 

      X = [1, 2, 3, 4]

Y = [] # define an empty storage list

for x in X:
    Y.append( x**2 )

print(X)
print(Y)

      [1, 2, 3, 4]
[1, 4, 9, 16]
    5. Calculating Data from Members of a List - A More Pythonic Way

      A more Pythonic method for calculating Y is to use a list comprehension. Here, we define a list using the brackets again, [ and ]. Inside the brackets, we include:

      1. an expression using a for-loop variable,
      2. the keyword for
      3. a declaration of the loop variable x
      4. the keyword in

      5. the iterable, X, over which we iterate It looks like the following: 

        X = [1, 2, 3, 4]

# Form an array of squared values (list comprehension)
Y = [x**2 for x in X]

print(X)
print(Y)

        [1, 2, 3, 4]
[1, 4, 9, 16]

        Notice the syntax for the list comprehension is very English-like: we're instructing Python to define a list Y whose elements are x**2 (x squared) for each x in the list X.

10.6.6. Level 5: Intermediate Python - Functions

  • Defining functions is a very powerful feature of many programming languages.
    • Functions allow for the automation of repeated tasks.
    • Complex tasks may be programmed once, and errors may be worked out.
    • Then, the finished function can be used repeatedly without having to code the process again.
    • This eliminates opportunities for error, and it saves the programmer time!
  • In Python, we define a function using the def keyword. We do the following:
    • Function header (first line)
      • Write def, followed by …
      • a function name, followed by …
      • any input arguments
      • Terminate using a colon (:)
    • Statements internal to the function
      • These are delineated by writing code with (at least) one level of indentation with respect to the function header
  • In a script block, we define the function first with no indentation for the function header.
    • Subsequent code following the function definition may then invoke the function.
  1. A Function as a Set of Commands

    A function may be as simple as a set of commands we wish to use and reuse. In this case, a function may not even require an input or provide any outputs. 

    def foo():
    '''
       This function, FOO, prints messages.
       It receives no inputs, nor does it
       return any functional outputs.
    '''
    print('Function (foo) command 1.')
    print('Function (foo) command 2.')

# The function definition is over, since we have reset
#   indentation back to zero.
# Subsequent commands may invoke foo
print('This is the first command outside the function foo.\n')

foo() # we invoke the function defined above

    This is the first command outside the function foo.

Function (foo) command 1.
Function (foo) command 2.
  2. A Function with a Single Input

    Here is a simple function with one input and zero outputs. 

    def square(x):
    '''
       square prints the square of its single input.
    '''
    print('{0} squared is {1}.'.format(x, x**2))

# The function definition is over, since we have reset
#   indentation back to zero.
# Subsequent commands may invoke square

z = 7
square(z)

# We can even use (and reuse) square
print('\nNow, let us use square in a FOR loop:')
for y in range(0,12):
    square(y)

    7 squared is 49.

Now, let us use square in a FOR loop:
0 squared is 0.
1 squared is 1.
2 squared is 4.
3 squared is 9.
4 squared is 16.
5 squared is 25.
6 squared is 36.
7 squared is 49.
8 squared is 64.
9 squared is 81.
10 squared is 100.
11 squared is 121.
  3. A Function with a Single Input and a Single Output

    Here is a simple function with one input and one output. 

    def cube(x):
    '''
       cube eturns the cube of its single input.
    '''

    return x**3

# The function definition is over, since we have reset
#   indentation back to zero.
# Subsequent commands may invoke square

z = 7
zc = cube(z)

# We can even use (and reuse) square
print(f'{z}^3 = {zc}')

    7^3 = 343
  4. A Function with a Single Input and Multiple Outputs

    Here is a simple function with one input and multiple outputs. Formally, a Python function can return only a single output. To extract multiple outputs, we can make the function return a list or an other iterable. Here, we use a tuple: 

    def bar(x):
    '''
       bar returns two values for a single input x:
         x**2.
    '''

    xsq = x**2

    # % is the modulo operator.
    #  Thus: x % b is x modulo b
    xsq_odd = x % 2 == 1

    # The return value value is a tuple containing
    # two elements
    return (xsq, xsq_odd)

# The function definition is over, since we have reset
#   indentation back to zero.
# Subsequent commands may invoke square

# There are two ways in which we can retrieve our output:
# METHOD 1: We can treat the output as a list/tuple/iterable.
out_list = bar(2)
print('For an input of 2, bar returns:')
print(out_list)

# METHOD 2: We can allow Python to "unpack" the tuple into
# distinct variables
z = 3
zc, zc_odd = bar(z)
print(f'\nz = {z}; z^2 = {zc}; z^2 = odd? {zc_odd}')

w = 4
wc, wc_odd = bar(w)
print(f'\nw = {w}; w^2 = {wc}; z^2 = odd? {wc_odd}')

    For an input of 2, bar returns:
(4, False)

z = 3; z^2 = 9; z^2 = odd? True

w = 4; w^2 = 16; z^2 = odd? False

10.6.7. Level 6: Classes

Classes are a very powerful programming concept. You get to define your own data type (a class) and define how variables of your custom type (objects) behave and interact. Classes and objects generalize the concepts of types and variables. This could be written in an analogy: variable:type::object:class

Classes are useful because we can define classes that use objects to represent real-world systems in a natural way. This is done in two major ways: (1) defining attributes for the class and/or objects of that class, and (2) defining behaviors for objects in the class. Behaviors are also called methods. These are simply functions that are associated with the class that can be used to manipulate or transform objects.

As an example, we might define a Person class to store information about various individuals. Each individual we wish to represent will have his or her own object. We would define in the class properties (attributes) that people have, such as a name and an age.

We have elementary data types, such as int, float, char, etc. We also have iterables (list, tuple, dict, etc.). Classes allow us to define objects which may be composite data types: objects in the class may have properties of various elementary types.

Let's learn how the basics of this very powerful concept here.

  1. Defining a Person Class

    To define a class, we need the basic parts.

    • Header line.
      • Every class definition begins with the keyword class.
      • The class name follows class, and typically begins with a capital letter, signifying that this is a user-defined class.
      • The class header ends in a colon (:)
    • Body

      • The body includes any code following the header delineated by at least one level of indentation deeper than the header. The following Person class has two attributes. These are defined in the __init__ method (function). 

        class Person:

    def __init__(self, name, age):
        self.name = name
        self.age = age

# Indentation is reset to end the class definition
#    Now, we use the class

me = Person('Ricky', '23') # define me as an object of cass Person

print(me) # print information about the object

# We can access object attributes (fields, properties) using
#   "dot" notation
print(me.name)
print(me.age)

        <__main__.Person object at 0x1014b1490>
Ricky
23

        We observe that classes are useful for storing related pieces of information in a custom-variable known as an object. Now, let us add another method to indicate how useful it is to define both properties and methods.

  2. Adding Another Method to the Person Class

    We write another method by defining this function (def introduce(self):) one level of indentation subordinate to the class definition. 

    class Person:

    def __init__(self, name, age):
        self.name = name
        self.age = age

    def introduce(self):
        print("I am {0}, and I'm {1} years old.".format(
                self.name, self.age))

# Indentation is reset to end the class definition
#    Now, we use the class

me = Person('Ricky', '23')

# Invoke the "introduce" method
me.introduce()

# Note: the syntax for calling a method is obj.method()

    I am Ricky, and I'm 23 years old.
